#### 2. Candidates, ranked by importance to the era's global story

##### LA-1. Latin American Catholicism, from the Mexican Revolution to Aparecida (1906–c. 2010)

- **Kind:** church body (a continental church row, as VIII.48 is).
- **Dates:** 1906–c. 2010. Real anchors inside the window: the Mexican Constitution of 1917 [S]; the suspension of public worship on 31 July 1926 (verified, below); CELAM founded at the first General Conference, Rio de Janeiro, 25 July–4 August 1955 (verified); Medellín, August–September 1968 (verified); Puebla 1979 (verified secondary); Santo Domingo 1992 [S]; Aparecida 2007 [S].
- **Region and countries:** Latin America; Mexico, Brazil, Colombia (CELAM's seat), Chile, Argentina, El Salvador, Peru, Cuba.
- **Lane:** 5 Latin West & Catholicism.
- **Section A:** no question (Catholic).
- **B1 Sourcing:** Rich. The bishops' own conference documents in Spanish and Portuguese, CEHILA's multi-volume history, a Cambridge survey, Meyer on Mexico. Asymmetry: episcopal voices dominate; IX.8 holds the poor's own voice (Solentiname).
- **B2 Ecology:** Full: church and state (Mexico, Cuba), devotion (Guadalupe, Aparecida as shrine and as the 2007 venue), the bishops' conferences, dictatorship and human rights, the Pentecostal and charismatic competition.
- **B3 Uniqueness:** High. It is the receiver for VIII.48, and the parent that IX.8 (base communities) and LA-4 (Cristeros) hang from. Without it the world's largest Catholic region has only a 1960s–1990s base-community row in era 10.
- **B4 Audiences:** Catholics of the region and diaspora, historians, pastors, seekers asking about the church and the poor.
- **B5 Scale:** the largest Catholic population of the century (tiebreak only).
- **Tier:** Tier 1.
- **Evidence:**
  1. *own text (charter)* — CELAM II, *La Iglesia en la actual transformación de América Latina a la luz del Concilio* (Medellín, 1968) ("The Church in the present-day transformation of Latin America in the light of the Council"). Already verified in the repo (Sources_Research_E, IX.8). Chapter 14, *Pobreza de la Iglesia* ("Poverty of the Church"), quoted in Boaventura Kloppenburg, "Opción preferencial por los pobres", *Medellín* (CELAM's journal), OCR text read this run — **verified (secondary)** — https://revistas.celam.org/index.php/medellin/article/download/1504/1412/3282
     - Original (Pobreza 1, as Kloppenburg quotes it): «El Episcopado Latinoamericano no puede quedar indiferente ante las tremendas injusticias sociales existentes en América Latina, que mantienen a la mayoría de nuestros pueblos en una dolorosa pobreza cercana en muchísimos casos a la inhumana miseria».
       *My rendering:* "The Latin American bishops cannot stay indifferent to the terrible social injustices in Latin America. They keep most of our peoples in painful poverty, in very many cases close to inhuman misery."
     - Original (Pobreza 2): «Un sordo clamor brota de millones de hombres, pidiendo a sus pastores una liberación que no les llega de ninguna parte».
       *My rendering:* "A muffled cry rises from millions of people. They ask their pastors for a liberation that reaches them from nowhere."
     - The OCR is noisy; the wording above is restored from it and must be re-checked against an edition of Medellín before any quote enters a record.
  2. *own text* — CELAM III, *Documento de Puebla* (1979), n. 89, quoted by Kloppenburg (same URL): the cry of the poor is now «claro, creciente, impetuoso y, en ocasiones, amenazante». *My rendering:* "clear, growing, forceful, and at times threatening." — **verified (secondary)**.
  3. *own act* — First General Conference of the Latin American Episcopate, Rio de Janeiro, 25 July–4 August 1955; CELAM set up under Pius XII; seat Bogotá; first president Cardinal Jaime de Barros Câmara. Benedict XVI's letter to CELAM's president, 14 May 2005 (in-era), recalls it. **verified** — https://www.vatican.va/content/benedict-xvi/pt/letters/2005/documents/hf_ben-xvi_let_20050514_celam.html ; https://encyclopedia.com/religion/encyclopedias-almanacs-transcripts-and-maps/consejo-episcopal-latinoamericano-celam . The papal approval date varies (29 June or 2 November 1955); leave it open.
  4. *reference work (Latin American scholarship)* — CEHILA (Comisión para el Estudio de la Historia de la Iglesia en América Latina, founded 1973 under Enrique Dussel), *Historia General de la Iglesia en América Latina* ("General History of the Church in Latin America"), Salamanca: Sígueme, 1981– (tomo VII 1981; tomo I/1 1983); Portuguese edition by Vozes [S on date]. **verified (founding 1973, publisher, two volume dates)** — https://revistas.unav.edu/index.php/scripta-theologica/article/download/16226/12525/53058 ; https://www.redalyc.org/pdf/3435/343529805014.pdf
  5. *reference work* — Ondina E. González and Justo L. González, *Christianity in Latin America: A History* (Cambridge: CUP, 2008), xii + 331 pp.; chapters include "Catholicism after Vatican II" and "Pentecostalism and autochthonous movements". **verified** — https://cambridge.org/core/journals/church-history/article/christianity-in-latin-america-a-history-by-ondina-e-gonzalez-and-justo-l-gonzalez-cambridge-cambridge-university-press-2008-xii-332pp-2399-paper/EF36BC6FC61EA3001B3B30C1D96D110D
  6. *reference work (later scholarship on in-era matters)* — Todd Hartch, *The Rebirth of Latin American Christianity* — review seen (Project MUSE); publisher and year (Oxford, 2014) **[S]** — https://muse.jhu.edu/article/604257/pdf
  7. Enrique Dussel, *Historia de la Iglesia en América Latina: coloniaje y liberación* (1972) — **[S]**.
- **Living today:** yes. Newest dated evidence: ACI Prensa, 2025, on the 25th anniversary of the Cristero martyrs' canonization (https://www.aciprensa.com/noticias/113547/en-eeuu-celebraran-25-anos-de-la-canonizacion-de-los-martires-mexicanos). Grade: CR (2025).
- **Overlap:** VIII.48 (predecessor, ends 1906). IX.8 (base communities; keep as child). IX.23 and IX.47 (Rome-centered; this row is the region). IX.7 (CCR). The Shared-Events list already has a Medellín 1968 event with no sources (Build/Ministry/Features/Shared-Events/Candidates.md line 133); item 1 above is a source for it.
- **Recommendation:** enter.
- **Gate question G-LA1. How should the Latin American Catholic church of 1906–2010 enter?**
  (a) One continental church row, 1906–c. 2010, receiving VIII.48, with IX.8 and the Cristero War as its children. (b) Two rows split at 1955 (before and after CELAM). (c) Country rows (Mexico, Brazil) only. (d) No row; widen IX.23's regions and add lines. **Recommended: (a).** It mirrors VIII.48's shape and closes the receiver gap V2.0 §1A named. (b) splits a story whose own documents (Rio 1955 to Aparecida 2007) read as one chain. (d) leaves 1906–1962 empty.

##### LA-2. Brazilian Pentecostalism — the three waves (1910–c. 2010)

- **Kind:** church family (bounded by country), told through named bodies: Congregação Cristã no Brasil (1910), Assembleias de Deus (1911), the 1950s wave [S], the Igreja Universal do Reino de Deus (1977).
- **Dates:** 1910–c. 2010.
- **Region and countries:** Latin America; Brazil (Belém, São Paulo, Santo Antônio da Platina, Rio de Janeiro).
- **Lane:** 7 Global Revival & Pentecostal.
- **Section A:** not yet assessable on a dated statement. All three named bodies are expected to be trinitarian, but no dated pre-2010 statement was seen. Wanted: a dated AD credo (for example the *Cremos* printed by CPAD) and the CCB's *Pontos de Doutrina e da Fé* [S].
- **B1:** Rich in Portuguese scholarship. The bodies' own press (*Mensageiro da Paz*, CGADB organ, verified as such) and a strong Brazilian sociology of religion (Freston, Mariano). Asymmetry: the CCB publishes little about itself.
- **B2:** Full: worship and glossolalia, Swedish and Italian-American founders, migration to the cities, the hymnal (*Harpa Cristã*, hymns by Frida Vingren), politics from the 1986 Constituent Assembly on, media and the IURD's style.
- **B3:** High. IX.11 is a multi-continent family that by its own rule cannot tell one body's story; Brazil is the largest Pentecostal story in the region. IX.11 does already hold part of it: its census record tells the Assembleias de Deus founding as a Tier 1 documented story, "Two Swedes Sail for Pará" (November 1910 – June 1911), citing the Igreja Batista de Belém records of June 1911 and Gedeon Freire de Alencar, *Assembleias de Deus: Origem, implantação e militância (1911–1946)* (São Paulo, 2010), as the standard Brazilian reference. It also names the IURD (1977, Macedo) as a voice, and its placeLabel is "Brazil · Nigeria and beyond". So LA-2 would carry what IX.11 tells only in part, and one story would be told twice unless G-132 rules which row keeps it.
- **B4:** Brazilian believers and diaspora, historians, pastors, seekers.
- **B5:** Very large (tiebreak only).
- **Tier:** Tier 1.
- **Evidence:**
  1. *own act* — Assembleia de Deus founded 18 June 1911 in Belém, Pará, by Daniel Berg and Gunnar Vingren, as the *Missão da Fé Apostólica* ("Apostolic Faith Mission"); renamed Assembleia de Deus seven years later. One outlier gives 10 June. **verified (date and founders; rename year [S])** — https://www.jornalopcao.com.br/artigo-de-opiniao-2/assembleia-de-deus-celebra-114-anos-de-fundacao-no-brasil-717552/
  2. *own text* — Luigi Francescon, letter from Santo Antônio da Platina to Rosina Francescon, April–June 1910, described as the CCB's "birth certificate"; deposit on Zenodo. **verified (existence, date range)**; contents not read — https://zenodo.org/records/5102715 . A competing account places the founding in São Paulo (Brás) — open.
  3. *own act* — IURD opened 9 July 1977, Rio de Janeiro, Edir Macedo (with R. R. Soares); first site given as a bandstand in Méier or Rua Suburbana, Abolição — open. **verified (date)** — https://www.infoescola.com/cristianismo/igreja-universal/
  4. *study (mark 4 form)* — Paul Freston, «Breve história do pentecostalismo brasileiro» ("A short history of Brazilian Pentecostalism"), in Alberto Antoniazzi et al., *Nem anjos nem demônios: interpretações sociológicas do pentecostalismo* ("Neither angels nor demons"), Petrópolis: Vozes, 1994, pp. 67–162. Source of the "three waves" (*três ondas*) typology. **verified** — https://www.maxwell.vrac.puc-rio.br/24054/24054_3.PDF
  5. *study* — Ricardo Mariano, *Neopentecostais: sociologia do novo pentecostalismo no Brasil* ("Neo-Pentecostals: a sociology of the new Pentecostalism in Brazil"), São Paulo: Loyola, 1999; 2nd ed. 2005. **verified** — https://www.redalyc.org/pdf/742/74230106.pdf
  6. *study* — Paul Freston, *Protestantes e política no Brasil: da Constituinte ao impeachment* ("Protestants and politics in Brazil"), doctoral thesis, Unicamp, 1993. **verified** — https://www.cartacapital.com.br/?p=388709
  7. *dissent on the typology* — Mariano and others call Freston's waves "a genealogy rather than a typology"; the dispute itself is part of the record. **verified (that it is debated)** — https://dialnet.unirioja.es/descarga/articulo/8926090.pdf
- **Living today:** yes. AD's 115th anniversary, June 2026 (https://www.jornalopcao.com.br/religiao/115-anos-da-assembleia-de-deus-no-brasil-834514/). Grade: CR (2026).
- **Overlap:** IX.11 holds the Pará story (1910–11) as a Tier 1 documented story on Alencar 2010, and names the IURD (1977) as a voice. Under G-91(a), that story moves to LA-2 by a named change order and IX.11 keeps a pointer line; under G-132(a), IX.11 keeps the story and LA-2 cross-references it. The proposal here is that LA-2 keeps the story, because it is Brazil's founding and LA-2 is the Brazil row, but which row keeps it is the gate's ruling (G-91, G-132). Separately, IX.11's dates read "1950s-c. 2010" while its copy dates the AD "from 1911"; that inconsistency is IX.11's own (G-135), flagged here, not touched.
- **Recommendation:** enter once Section A is settled on a dated statement, for example a dated CGADB/CPAD *Cremos* or the CCB's *Pontos de Doutrina e da Fé*.
- **Gate question G-LA2. Where does Brazil's Pentecostal century go?** (a) Its own row, 1910–c. 2010, with the IURD as its third-wave closing line; IX.11's Pará story moves here by a named change order and IX.11 keeps one pointer line. (b) Named lines in IX.11 only, and IX.11's start moves to 1910. (c) Two rows: classical (CCB, AD, 1910–) and neo-Pentecostal (IURD, 1977–). **Recommended: (a).** IX.11's own rule asks for a bounded window, one country. One row matches the Brazilian scholarship, which tells the waves as one story; (c) would rest on a typology that scholars dispute (item 7).

##### LA-3. Chilean Pentecostalism — the Methodist Pentecostal Church and its daughters (1909–c. 2010)

- **Kind:** church body (Iglesia Metodista Pentecostal, IMP), with its daughter churches as named lines.
- **Dates:** 1909 (revival in the Methodist Episcopal church at Valparaíso) / 1910 (separation) – c. 2010.
- **Region and countries:** Latin America; Chile (Valparaíso, Santiago — Jotabeche, the "Catedral Evangélica").
- **Lane:** 7 Global Revival & Pentecostal.
- **Section A:** not yet assessable. Methodist in origin, so trinitarian is expected, but no dated IMP statement was seen, and one academic study says that this set of Chilean churches has no founding doctrinal documents at all (https://dialnet.unirioja.es/descarga/articulo/9713916.pdf, seen in a search summary only, [S] on wording). Wanted: the IMP's registered constitution, or a dated *Voz Pentecostal* statement.
- **B1:** Moderate-rich. Hoover's own history; the movement's periodical; Lalive d'Epinay's classic study; Pew 2006 overview; Chilean scholarship (Mansilla, Sepúlveda, Salas on the schisms).
- **B2:** Full: street preaching, the hacienda-style pastorate (Lalive's reading), the 1930s and 1950s schisms, the Te Deum Evangélico and church-state relations, the ecumenical turn of two daughters.
- **B3:** High. The earliest Latin American Pentecostalism that was national from its first years, not a mission plant. Two of its daughters, the Iglesia Pentecostal de Chile and the Misión Iglesia Pentecostal, were admitted to the WCC at New Delhi in 1961 — the first Pentecostal churches to join. That ties this row to IX.46.
- **B4:** Chilean believers, Pentecostal historians, ecumenists, sociologists of religion.
- **B5:** Large for its country (tiebreak only).
- **Tier:** Tier 2 (high).
- **Evidence:**
  1. *own text* — Willis C. Hoover, *Historia del avivamiento pentecostal en Chile* ("History of the Pentecostal revival in Chile"), Valparaíso: Imprenta Excélsior; dated 1931 by one source, 1948 by another, 1930 by a third; ET by Mario G. Hoover, 2000. **verified (existence); date open** — https://dialnet.unirioja.es/descarga/articulo/5513160.pdf . Rights: Hoover died in 1936 [S]; check before any vendoring.
  2. *reference* — Pew Forum, "Historical Overview of Pentecostalism in Chile" (5 October 2006): the 1909 revival, the 1910 break, the later name, the 1961 WCC entry of two daughters. **verified** — https://www.pewresearch.org/religion/2006/10/05/historical-overview-of-pentecostalism-in-chile/
  3. *study (mark 4 form)* — Christian Lalive d'Epinay, *El refugio de las masas: estudio sociológico del protestantismo chileno* ("The refuge of the masses"), Santiago: Editorial del Pacífico, 1968; fieldwork 1965–66; ET *Haven of the Masses* (London: Lutterworth, 1969) **[S on the ET]**. **verified (Spanish edition)** — https://www.redalyc.org/pdf/4075/407548401003.pdf ; https://crear.cl/wp-content/uploads/2014/03/Cuaderno-Investigacion-Social-35.pdf
  4. *own act* — WCC, New Delhi, 1961: Iglesia Pentecostal de Chile and Misión Iglesia Pentecostal admitted, with eight churches voting against each. **verified** — https://christianitytoday.com/1961/12/new-delhi-wcc-imc-merger-becomes-official
  5. *study* — Juan Sepúlveda, "Theological Characteristics of an Indigenous Pentecostalism: Chile", book chapter on Religion Online. **verified (existence)**; book and date [S] — https://www.religion-online.org/?p=9151
- **Living today:** yes. Bishop Edmundo Zenteno, IMP president, at a Te Deum Evangélico attended by President Boric (year not shown, before March 2026) — https://www.t13.cl/noticia/nacional/obispo-zenteno-llamo-al-dialogo-te-deum-reconciliacion-no-significa-aniquilar-al-otro ; Jotabeche's church regulation, 2024 — https://jotabeche.cl/wp-content/uploads/2024/06/Reglamento-Eclesiastico-Catedral-Evangelica-de-Chile.pdf . Grade: CR (2024).
- **Overlap:** IX.2 ("influence that reached as far as Chile by 1909", via Minnie Abrams) — keep as IX.2's line; this row is what grew. IX.11 does not name Chile.
- **Recommendation:** enter (Section A to be settled first).
- **Gate question G-LA3.** (a) Its own row, 1909–c. 2010, Tier 2. (b) A named line in IX.2 or IX.11. (c) One "Latin American classical Pentecostalism" row joining Chile and Brazil. **Recommended: (a)**, once a dated statement settles Section A. The two national stories differ in origin (a Methodist schism against Swedish and Italian missions) and scholars study them apart; (c) would break IX.11's one-country rule.

##### LA-4. The Cristero War (Cristiada), 1926–1929

- **Kind:** a Catholic movement (civil and armed), not a church body. Four marks:
  1. *Beginning:* the bishops' suspension of public worship on 31 July 1926, the day the Calles Law took effect (verified, below).
  2. *Carriers:* the Liga Nacional Defensora de la Libertad Religiosa (founded March 1925), the rural *cristero* fighters, the women's brigades [S].
  3. *Name:* *cristeros*, from their cry «¡Viva Cristo Rey!» [S on the cry as a founding text].
  4. *Recognition by historians:* Jean Meyer, *La Cristiada* (3 vols).
- **Dates:** 1926–1929 (the *arreglos* of June 1929). A second, smaller rising in the 1930s [S]. The Church's canonization of 25 martyrs on 21 May 2000 falls in the era.
- **Region and countries:** Latin America; Mexico (Jalisco, Michoacán, Guanajuato, Zacatecas, Colima).
- **Lane:** 5 Latin West & Catholicism.
- **Section A:** no question (Catholic).
- **B1:** Rich, and unusually self-documenting at the grass roots: Meyer worked from veterans' interviews and their own papers [S on method]. Asymmetry: state and League records against rural fighters' voices.
- **B2:** Full: worship suspended, priests hidden, rural war, martyrdom, the settlement's bitterness, memory and the 2000 canonization.
- **B3:** High. It is the century's largest armed Catholic rising in the Americas. It is a different thing from LA-1's institutional story.
- **B4:** Mexican Catholics and diaspora, historians, readers asking about religion and violence.
- **B5:** Tiebreak only.
- **Tier:** Tier 2.
- **Evidence:**
  1. *event* — suspension of public worship, 31 July 1926, the day the Calles Law took effect; the League founded March 1925; *arreglos* June 1929 with amnesty and non-enforcement. **verified (secondary)** — https://www.aciprensa.com/noticias/127285/guerra-cristera-mexico-100-anos-suspension-culto ; https://dialnet.unirioja.es/descarga/articulo/8306139.pdf . (No original-language sentence is quoted here: the wording seen came through a search summary, not a page read.)
  2. *study (mark 4)* — Jean Meyer, *La Cristiada*, México: Siglo XXI, 1973–74, trans. Aurelio Garzón del Camino: I *La guerra de los cristeros* (1973), II *El conflicto entre la Iglesia y el Estado* (1973 or 1974), III *Los cristeros* (1974). More than 20 reprints. ET *The Cristero Rebellion* (Cambridge UP) [S on year]. **verified** — https://historicas.unam.mx/publicaciones/publicadigital/libros/escribir/ELH_020.pdf ; https://resolve.cambridge.org/core/books/cristero-rebellion/preface/E946062FB7D62BFC21C002E6829F26E5
  3. *own text (church, in-era)* — John Paul II, homily for the canonization of Cristóbal Magallanes and 24 companions, 21 May 2000. **verified** — https://www.vatican.va/content/john-paul-ii/es/homilies/2000/documents/hf_jp-ii_hom_20000521_canonizations.pdf
  4. *own founding text* — the League's 1925 manifesto or program — **wanted, not found**.
- **Living today:** the war ended; the memory and the cult of its martyrs continue: 25th-anniversary Masses, 2025 (ACI Prensa link in LA-1). Grade: CR (2025). Living flag: false for the war, as an episode; whether the cult carries it is Mark's call (as IX.1's precedent).
- **Overlap:** none in the census. VIII.48 carries the Reforma of the 1850s, the root of the conflict.
- **Recommendation:** enter once one dated own text of the movement is staged (for example the Liga Nacional Defensora de la Libertad Religiosa's 1925 program or a dated *cristero* manifesto), as LA-1's child; or a named line in LA-1. The bishops' suspension of worship (1926) is an episcopal act, and the 2000 homily is a later church text; neither is the movement's own founding text, which the evidence rule needs.
- **Gate question G-LA4.** (a) Its own row, 1926–1929, living false, closing on the 2000 canonization. (b) A named line inside LA-1. (c) Its own row 1926–c. 1938 including the second rising. **Recommended: (a), once one dated own text of the movement is staged.** It shows all four marks on verified items and has Meyer's standard study, and the roster already takes episode rows (the Confessing Church, 1933–45). But its own founding text (item 4) is wanted, and the episode-row precedent does not waive the evidence rule. Until the text is staged, (b) is the fallback. (c) rests on an [S] date.

##### LA-5. Protestantism in Latin America — from Panama 1916 to CLAI and the Latin American Theological Fraternity (1916–c. 2010)

- **Kind:** church family (historic, mission and immigrant Protestant churches), with cross-church bodies as its dated acts.
- **Dates:** 1916 (Congress on Christian Work in Latin America, Panama) – c. 2010.
- **Region and countries:** Latin America; Panama, Mexico, Brazil, Argentina, Peru, Colombia, Bolivia, Guatemala.
- **Lane:** 6 Protestant & Evangelical.
- **Section A:** per body, never on the label. The historic bodies (Presbyterian, Methodist, Baptist, Lutheran, Anglican) carry dated classical confessions. No family-level question.
- **B1:** Moderate-rich: the 1916 congress reports (3 vols, 1917), Bastian's histories, CLADE and FTL papers, Garrard-Burnett on Guatemala.
- **B2:** Full: mission schools, liberal politics and Freemasonry (Bastian), immigrant Lutherans, the ecumenical/evangelical split (CLAI vs CONELA), integral mission, the Guatemalan war years.
- **B3:** Moderate-high. IX.24 carries only the Lausanne thread (Padilla, Escobar) and IX.46 only Geneva. Nothing tells the region's own Protestant story.
- **B4:** Latin American Protestants, historians, mission students.
- **B5:** Tiebreak only.
- **Tier:** Tier 2.
- **Evidence:**
  1. *own act* — Congress on Christian Work in Latin America, Panama City, 10–20 February 1916, organized by the Committee on Cooperation in Latin America (formed 1913) because Edinburgh 1910 had left Latin America out; 304 delegates and visitors from 21 nations; few Latin Americans; reports 1917, 3 vols. **verified** — https://weamc.global/wp-content/uploads/2016/09/Halls-Tim-Panama-1916-background-.pdf ; https://wcc2006.info/en/about-the-assembly/latin-america-brazil/ecumenical-history-of-latin-america.html ; https://commons.ptsem.edu/id/christianworkinl33cong
  2. *own act* — CLADE I (I Congreso Latinoamericano de Evangelización), Bogotá, 21–30 November 1969. **verified** — https://www.christianitytoday.com/1969/12/bogot-latin-liaison/
  3. *own act* — Fraternidad Teológica Latinoamericana (Latin American Theological Fraternity), first consultation at Cochabamba, 1970 (November in one source; December [S]). **verified (1970, Cochabamba)** — https://ru.dgb.unam.mx/bitstream/20.500.14330/TES01000133518/3/0133518.pdf
  4. *own act* — Consejo Latinoamericano de Iglesias (CLAI): Huampaní meeting November 1977, Oaxtepec assembly September 1978 (CLAI "en formación"), constituted at Huampaní 1982 (WCC page says 1983 — open); CONELA formed as the conservative alternative. **verified** — https://www.christianitytoday.com/1979/02/current-religious-thought-strides-toward-unity-in-latin/ ; https://uia.org/s/or/en/1100058659 ; https://christianitytoday.com/ct/1981/june-12/latin-believers-plan-for-group-to-voice-majority.html
  5. *study (Latin American scholarship)* — Jean-Pierre Bastian, *Historia del protestantismo en América Latina* ("History of Protestantism in Latin America"), México: CUPSA (Casa Unida de Publicaciones), 1990; and *Protestantismos y modernidad latinoamericana: historia de unas minorías religiosas activas en América Latina* ("Protestantisms and Latin American modernity"), México: FCE, 1994, trans. of *Le protestantisme en Amérique latine* (Labor et Fides, 1994). **verified** — https://catalogobiblio.unach.cl/vufind/Record/DIGFCE0301/Details ; https://theopro.unistra.fr/websites/theopro/Doc_Anciens_Enseignants/Bastian_JP__liste_des_Publications_2026.pdf
  6. *study* — Virginia Garrard-Burnett, *Protestantism in Guatemala: Living in the New Jerusalem* (Austin: University of Texas Press, 1998), xvi + 248 pp. **verified** — https://cambridge.org/core/journals/americas/article/abs/protestantism-in-guatemala-living-in-the-new-jerusalem-by-virginia-garrardburnett-austin-university-of-texas-press-1998-pp-248-notes-bibliography-index-3000/B7FBD789A1078319B0B7BFAC240B516B
  7. *study* — Paul Freston, *Evangelicals and Politics in Asia, Africa and Latin America* (Cambridge UP, 2001). **verified** — https://www.americamagazine.org/books/2001/09/24/more-bible-thumping/
  8. ISAL (Iglesia y Sociedad en América Latina, 1961; Rubem Alves, José Míguez Bonino) — **[S]**; David Stoll, *Is Latin America Turning Protestant?* (1990) — **[S]**.
- **Living today:** yes (CLAI and FTL continue, [S] on newest date; Global Ministries page undated). Grade: none (no dated item after 2010); a dated item is on the wanted list.
- **Overlap:** IX.24 (Padilla/Escobar at Lausanne 1974 — keep there; this row holds the FTL's own Latin American story before and after). IX.46 (WCC). LA-2 and LA-3 (Pentecostal, separate).
- **Recommendation:** enter.
- **Gate question G-LA5.** (a) One family row, 1916–c. 2010, Tier 2. (b) Named lines in IX.24 (FTL, CLADE) and IX.46 (Panama 1916, CLAI). (c) Hold until a dated living item and an ISAL source are verified. **Recommended: (a).** Its dated acts are verified and Bastian is the field's standard; (b) would scatter one region's story across two North-Atlantic rows.

##### LA-6. The Catholic Charismatic Renewal in Latin America (from 1969)

- **Kind:** a Catholic renewal movement, already carried by IX.7.
- **Dates:** Brazil 1969 (Campinas) – c. 2010.
- **Region:** Latin America; Brazil, Mexico, Guatemala, Colombia.
- **Lane:** 5 Latin West & Catholicism (IX.7's).
- **Section A:** no question.
- **Four marks:** beginning (Campinas 1969, Jesuits Haroldo Rahm and Edward Dougherty — verified secondary); carriers (prayer groups, later Canção Nova [S]); name (*Renovação Carismática Católica*); recognition (Chesnut ch. 4; Carranza [S]).
- **Tier:** n/a (line).
- **Evidence:**
  1. RCC brought to Campinas in 1969 by Pe. Haroldo Joseph Rahm and Pe. Eduardo Dougherty, after Carranza's dissertation — **verified (secondary)** — https://revistaplura.emnuvens.com.br/anais/article/view/545/388 . Brenda Carranza, *Renovação Carismática Católica: origens, mudanças e tendências* (2000) — **[S]**.
  2. R. Andrew Chesnut, *Competitive Spirits: Latin America's New Religious Economy* (Oxford UP, 2003), ch. 4, "A Preferential Option for the Spirit: The Catholic Charismatic Renewal"; focused on Brazil, Mexico and Guatemala. **verified** — https://biblioteca.izt.uam.mx/vufind/Record/EBSCO_ocn560166420/TOC
- **Living today:** yes; newest dated item not found. Grade: none yet.
- **Overlap:** IX.7 carries the CCR (Duquesne 1967; Suenens) but lists no Latin American region.
- **Cross-reference:** GS-A6 places El Shaddai, also a Catholic charismatic movement, by a different rule: it moves out of IX.11 into a national Catholic row. G-133 asks for one rule.
- **Recommendation:** named line in IX.7, with Latin America added to its regions.
- **Gate question G-LA6.** (a) A named line in IX.7 and Latin America added to its regions. (b) Its own row, 1969–c. 2010, on Chesnut. (c) A line in LA-1. **Recommended: (a).** IX.7 already holds the CCR as one of its four movements; a separate row would split one movement by continent.

##### LA-7. The Caribbean churches in the twentieth century — autonomy and the Caribbean Conference of Churches (1906–c. 2010)

- **Kind:** church family (Anglican, Methodist, Baptist, Moravian, Presbyterian and Catholic churches of the English-speaking Caribbean).
- **Dates:** 1906–c. 2010; anchors: MCCA autonomy, 18 May 1967; CCC inaugurated November 1973, Kingston.
- **Region:** Caribbean (census region likely "Latin America" or "North America" — VIII.46 Haiti uses "North America"; a mapping call); Jamaica, Trinidad and Tobago, Barbados, Antigua, Guyana.
- **Lane:** 6 Protestant & Evangelical.
- **Section A:** no question (historic confessions).
- **B1:** Moderate to 1962 (Dayfoot), Thin after it on verified items. Kortright Davis (1990) and Idris Hamid's *Troubling of the Waters* (1973) [S].
- **B2:** Full in principle: emancipation churches, independence and autonomy, Caribbean theology, Revival worship next door.
- **B3:** High. No Caribbean church row exists after 1815 except Haiti to 1860.
- **B4:** Caribbean Christians and diaspora (IX.25 already notes Caribbean congregants in London), historians.
- **Tier:** Tier 2 if sourced; else Tier 3 now.
- **Evidence:**
  1. *own act* — deed of separation from the British Methodist Conference signed in Antigua, 18 May 1967; Hugh Sherlock first president of the MCCA. **verified** — https://www.pressreader.com/jamaica/jamaica-gleaner/20170520/281917363016068 ; https://mccaconference.org/history/
  2. *own act* — Caribbean Conference of Churches inaugurated November 1973 in Jamaica, out of CADEC and ARC; Archbishop Samuel Carter among its first three presidents. **verified (1973, Jamaica)**; Catholic membership **[S]** — https://barbadostoday.bb/2024/02/27/revive-ccc-for-the-love-of-god ; https://www.pressreader.com/jamaica/jamaica-gleaner/20190825/282471415520751
  3. *study* — Arthur Charles Dayfoot, *The Shaping of the West Indian Church, 1492–1962* (Gainesville: University Press of Florida; Kingston: The Press UWI, 1999), xvii + 360 pp. **verified** — https://www.cambridge.org/core/journals/journal-of-latin-american-studies/article/arthur-charles-dayfoot-the-shaping-of-the-west-indian-church-14921962-gainesville-fl-university-press-of-florida-1999-pp-xvii360-4995-hb/47043940EBC00D7DB13A02BBCE842BB0
  4. Kortright Davis, *Emancipation Still Comin'* (Orbis, 1990) — **[S]** (search found nothing).
- **Living today:** MCCA yes (site; Gleaner 2017). CCC weakened: a 2024 column calls for its revival. Grade: CR (2024).
- **Overlap:** IX.52 Anglican (no Caribbean region); IX.46 WCC; IX.25 (diaspora in Britain).
- **Recommendation:** hold for sources.
- **Gate question G-LA7.** (a) Enter now, 1906–c. 2010, Tier 2, on Dayfoot, the MCCA and the CCC. (b) Hold until a post-1962 study is verified (Davis 1990 or another), then enter. (c) Named lines in IX.52 and IX.46 only. **Recommended: (b).** Dayfoot stops at 1962, and the half-century after independence rests on two institutional dates alone.

##### LA-8. Afro-Caribbean Christian churches — the Spiritual (Shouter) Baptists of Trinidad, and Jamaican Revival

- **Kind:** church family (independent / initiated).
- **Dates:** Trinidad: Shouters Prohibition Ordinance passed 16 November 1917 (commencement 28 November given elsewhere), repealed 30 March 1951; Liberation Day a public holiday from 1996. Jamaica: Bedward's Jamaica Native Baptist Free Church to his arrest in April 1921 (the movement began 1889 — era 9).
- **Region:** Caribbean; Trinidad and Tobago, St Vincent, Grenada, Jamaica.
- **Lane:** 7 Global Revival & Pentecostal (as IX.26 Ratana) or 4-style "initiated" — a lane call.
- **Section A:** **not yet assessable.** No dated statement of faith was found. Some congregations share worshippers and practice with Orisha [S]; under A5 the world is judged on its established strands as a whole. Bedward's own claims about himself [S] would need reading before any Jamaican line.
- **B1:** Thin-moderate on verified items: the colonial ordinance, the state's own Liberation Day messages; studies [S] (Glazier, Zane).
- **B2:** Rich in principle: mourning and "spiritual travel", baptism, persecution and liberation.
- **B3:** High: the only Afro-Caribbean Christian church in the region's twentieth century with a dated persecution and a dated freedom.
- **Tier:** Tier 3 (deferred) until A and B1 are settled.
- **Evidence:**
  1. Ordinance 1917, repeal 30 March 1951, holiday 1996 (Panday government). **verified** — https://obeahhistories.org/shouters-prohibition-ordinance/ ; https://otp.tt/message-on-spiritual-baptist-liberation-shouter-day-2024
  2. Stephen D. Glazier's Trinidad field recordings 1976–79, *Spiritual Baptist Music of Trinidad* (Smithsonian Folkways, 1980). **verified** — https://folkways-media.si.edu/docs/folkways/artwork/FW04234.pdf . His monograph *Marchin' the Pilgrims Home* (1983) — **[S]** (not found). Wallace Zane, *Journeys to the Spiritual Lands* (Oxford, 1999) — **[S]** (not found).
  3. Bedward: 1921 march and arrest; committed to an asylum; died 1930. Fullest study: Gosse (UWI Press, 2022) [S on author and title]. **verified (events, secondary)** — https://jamaica-gleaner.com/article/commentary/20220908/michael-allen-reviewing-alexander-bedward
- **Living today:** yes — President of Trinidad and Tobago, Liberation Day message, 2024 (otp.tt link above). Grade: CD (2024), a dated state text.
- **Overlap:** none. IX.4 (AICs) is Africa only.
- **Recommendation:** hold for sources (Section A first).
- **Gate question G-LA8.** (a) Hold: put a dated Spiritual Baptist statement of faith (or a study that sets one out) and Zane or Glazier on the wanted list; enter Tier 2 once A clears. (b) Enter now, A settled by Mark under A5. (c) A named line inside LA-7. **Recommended: (a).** The persecution story is dated and verified, but Section A cannot be judged on anything seen, and the floor is Mark's.

##### LA-9. Mexican Oneness Pentecostalism — Iglesia Apostólica de la Fe en Cristo Jesús (1914)

- **Kind:** church body (register family, IX.15).
- **Evidence:** Romanita Carbajal (Carvajal) de Valenzuela, converted among Pentecostals in Los Angeles, returned to Villa Aldama, Chihuahua, and converted twelve relatives on 1 November 1914 (one source); Miguel García led after her; spread to Sinaloa (1925) and Tijuana (1926); the US sister body incorporated 1930. **verified (1914, founder, place; day [S])** — https://dialnet.unirioja.es/descarga/articulo/3088702.pdf ; https://aaofcj.com/wp-content/uploads/2024/04/Brief-History-Los-Angeles-District.pdf (own text of the US sister church).
- **Section A:** the register's own (Oneness); assessed in IX.15.
- **Overlap:** IX.15's relations line already names "the same 1914 Mexican Apostolic revival" but its regions are North America only; IX.16 (La Luz del Mundo) descends from it.
- **Recommendation:** named line in IX.15, with Mexico added to its countries.
- **Gate question G-LA9.** (a) Named line in IX.15 and Mexico in its countries. (b) Its own register row. (c) No change. **Recommended: (a).** IX.15 already points to it; the line would name what the pointer refers to.

##### LA-10. The Ethiopian Orthodox Church in the Caribbean (1970)

- **Kind:** church body (Oriental Orthodox).
- **Evidence:** established in Jamaica in May 1970 by Archimandrite Laike Mariam Mandefro (later Archbishop Yesehaq), after Haile Selassie's visit (1966; one source 1968) and Rastafari requests. **verified (May 1970, founder)** — https://www.jamaicaobserver.com/2022/12/02/culture-minister-meets-with-archbishop-of-the-ethiopian-orthodox-church ; study: "By the Waters of Babylon: Ethiopian Orthodox Enculturation in a Rastafari Context, 1965–1980" (journal article, author [S]) — https://www.ebsco.com/articles/biography/162ed1a7-f468-587d-bd2a-9fb79256bbf2/by-the-waters-of-babylon-ethiopian-orthodox-enculturation-in-a-rastafari-context-1965-1980/
- **Living:** yes — Jamaica Observer 2022. Grade: CR (2022).
- **Overlap:** IX.20 (Modern Ethiopian & Eritrean Christianity); VIII.49's legacy already names Ethiopia as an image for Caribbean movements.
- **Recommendation:** named line in IX.20, with Jamaica added to its countries.
- **Gate question G-LA10.** (a) Named line in IX.20. (b) Named line in LA-7. (c) No line. **Recommended: (a)**: it is the Ethiopian church's own Western Hemisphere mission.

##### LA-11. Haiti, 1906–2010

- **Gap:** VIII.46 (1804–1860) has no successor; its legacy names "the anti-superstition drives of the 1940s". Era 10 holds nothing on Haiti: the *campagne anti-superstitieuse* (1941–42) [S], Duvalier and the church [S], *Ti Legliz* and Aristide [S], Protestant growth [S].
- **Evidence:** Anne Greene, *The Catholic Church in Haiti: Political and Social Change* (East Lansing, 1993) is already cited in VIII.46 (repo text). No French or Haitian Creole own text verified this run.
- **Recommendation:** hold for sources; when sourced, a named line in LA-1 (Catholic) or its own row.
- **Gate question G-LA11.** (a) Hold, with Greene and a French-language source (for example Laënnec Hurbon's work [S]) on the wanted list. (b) Named line in LA-1 now, on Greene. (c) Nothing. **Recommended: (a).**

##### LA-12. Central American evangelical growth (Guatemala)

- **Evidence:** Garrard-Burnett 1998 (verified, LA-5 item 6): few converts until the 1960s; militarization and violence weakened traditional ties. Ríos Montt and the Iglesia El Verbo (1982) [S].
- **Recommendation:** named line in LA-5 (or IX.11 if LA-5 is not entered).
- **Gate question G-LA12.** (a) Line in LA-5. (b) Line in IX.11. **Recommended: (a).**

- **Gap:** large Arab and Armenian immigrant churches in Brazil, Argentina and Mexico [S]. Nothing verified this run.
- **Recommendation:** hold for sources; when sourced, lines in IX.27 (Armenian) and IX.19 (Syriac).

#### 3. Out of scope (stated, so the reasoning is kept)

- **Rastafari (Jamaica, 1930–).** It grew from Jamaican Christianity and reads the Bible, but it holds Haile Selassie to be divine [S on the formulation]; on its plain confession it fails A1 (it is not Christ's divinity in Article 4's sense). Not a row. Its encounter with Christianity is LA-10's line.
- **Vodou, Santería/Regla de Ocha, Candomblé, Umbanda, Kardecist Spiritism.** Not Christian movements under A1, though Catholic practice is woven through several. Chesnut (2003) treats Candomblé and Vodou as religious "producers" competing with Pentecostals and the CCR — a fact for LA-1 and LA-2's lived world, not a row.
- **Liberation theology as its own row.** Prior ruling (V2.0 §1B, school-of-thought rule): a line in IX.8. Not re-proposed. If LA-1 enters, Medellín and Puebla move to LA-1 as institutional acts and IX.8 keeps the theology and the communities. IX.8's longDescription carries Medellín 1968 and Puebla, so this is a change to an existing row; G-90(a) states it so the ruling covers it.
- **Base communities.** IX.8 carries them.
- **La Luz del Mundo.** IX.16 carries it.
- **Legionaries of Christ (Mexico, 1941), Sodalitium (Peru, 1971), Schoenstatt in Chile.** Prior ruling (V2.0 §1B): new ecclesial movements are lines in IX.7 / IX.47. Not re-proposed; [S].
- **Mennonite colonies in Paraguay (1927–) and Mexico.** An Anabaptist river story; era 10 has no Anabaptist row to receive it. Flag for whichever sweep covers the Anabaptist river; [S].
- **US Latino Pentecostalism (Olazábal and others).** North American region; for that sweep.

#### Candidates, ranked by importance to the era's global story

##### 1. GS-A1 — Korean Protestantism in the twentieth century

- **Kind:** church family (one national family of church bodies; assessed as a church row)
- **Dates:** 1907-c. 2010. 1907: the first independent Presbytery (독노회) adopts the Twelve Articles on 17 September 1907, the same year as the Pyongyang revival IX.2 already carries.
- **Region / countries:** Greater Asia — South Korea, North Korea (to 1945/1950)
- **Lane:** 6 Protestant & Evangelical
- **Section A:** Clears on a dated statement. The Twelve Articles (12신조), adopted by the Presbyterian Church in Korea's first presbytery on 17 September 1907, Art. III: "In the Godhead, there are three Persons, the Father, the Son and the Holy Spirit, and these three are one God, the same in substance, equal in power and glory." English text as printed in Kim (2019), verified; the Korean wording of the 1907 text was not seen in this run. The articles were taken over from the Presbyterian Church in India's creed of 1904 (Kim 2019).
- **B1:** Strong. Two university-press studies verified (Lee 2010; Oak 2013, to 1915) plus Kim & Kim (CUP 2015), whose reliability is contested by one review (Railton, JEH): use it with that flag. Korean-language standard: 한국기독교역사연구소 (Institute for Korean Church History), 한국기독교의 역사 I-III; vol. III (since 1945) confirmed as translated into English in 2017; volume dates [S].
- **B2:** Strong. Dawn prayer (새벽기도), fasting prayer, the March First Movement, the shrine crisis and its martyrs, post-war schisms, a theology of the oppressed (minjung) and a growth economy: most of the eight lenses.
- **B3:** High. The roster has the 1907 revival (IX.2, ending 1910) and one megachurch (Yoido, inside IX.11), but no row for the church family that became the century's most-cited case of Asian Protestant growth.
- **B4:** Broad: historically interested learners, pastors (prayer practice, growth and its costs), thoughtful believers (shrine worship and complicity), academic users.
- **B5:** Large; tiebreak only.
- **Tier:** Tier 1
- **Evidence:**
  - 대한예수교장로회 신경 (12신조), 1907 — 'Creed of the Presbyterian Church in Korea (Twelve Articles)', adopted 17 Sept 1907 at 장대현교회, Pyongyang — *founding text* — **[S]** — Date and place from a Korean summary of 한국교회사학회지 60 (2021), DOI 10.22254/kchs.2021.60.02, seen only as a search summary. Korean original not seen.
  - H. Kim, 'A Reformed perspective of the Presbyterian Church in Korea's adoption of their "Twelve Articles of Faith" in 1907', MA thesis, North-West University, 2019 (prints the English text of the articles) — *scholarly study (thesis)* — **verified** — <http://hdl.handle.net/10394/33679>
  - Timothy S. Lee, Born Again: Evangelicalism in Korea (Honolulu: University of Hawai'i Press, 2010) — *scholarly study* — **verified** — <https://cambridge.org/core/journals/church-history/article/born-again-evangelicalism-in-korea-by-timothy-s-lee-honolulu-university-of-hawaii-press-2010-xvi-228-pp-4000-cloth/0F9A0A43787ECD82346F41D228A3E80C>
  - Sung-Deuk Oak, The Making of Korean Christianity: Protestant Encounters with Korean Religions, 1876-1915 (Waco: Baylor University Press, 2013) — *scholarly study (opening decade only)* — **verified** — <https://muse.jhu.edu/article/546988/>
  - Sebastian C. H. Kim and Kirsteen Kim, A History of Korean Christianity (Cambridge: CUP, 2015) — *scholarly survey* — **verified** — <https://ci.nii.ac.jp/ncid/BB1770886X> — A Journal of Ecclesiastical History review (Railton) alleges many factual errors; a Journal of Korean Religions review praises it. Use with that flag.
  - 한국기독교역사연구소 (Institute for Korean Church History), 한국기독교의 역사 I-III — 'A History of Christianity in Korea', vol. III (since 1945) translated as A History of Christianity in Korea since 1945 (2017) — *regional scholarship, Korean* — **[S]** — <https://chhouse.kr/product/한국기독교의역사제3호영문판-a-history-of-christianity-in-korea-since-1945/25622> — Existence of the series and of the 2017 translation of vol. III seen; volume dates and editions not confirmed.
  - Commission on Theological Concerns of the Christian Conference of Asia (ed.), Minjung Theology: People as the Subjects of History (CTC-CCA 1981; Orbis 1983), from the 1979 Asian Theological Conference — *founding text of the minjung line* — **[S]** — Edition dates conflict across catalogs.
  - Korean Declaration of Independence, 1 March 1919: 16 of the 33 signatories Protestant — *dated event* — **[S]** — Counts consistent across encyclopedic sources only.
  - 27th General Assembly, Pyongyang, September 1938, endorses shrine attendance; Chu Ki-chol (주기철) dies in custody 21 April 1944 — *dated events* — **[S]** — Britannica and encyclopedic sources; the 1938 resolution's Korean text was not found in this run.
- **Quotes (original, then rendering):**
  - (English (the Korean 1907 wording not seen)) “In the Godhead, there are three Persons, the Father, the Son and the Holy Spirit, and these three are one God, the same in substance, equal in power and glory.” — Kim 2019, art. III as printed (also the text of the 1904 Indian creed) — **verified against the downloaded thesis**
- **Living today:** True; newest dated evidence: 2025-05 (memorial opening reported); Kosin's 2011 constitution and Tonghap's 1971 revision of the creed are reported in the 2021 Korean article summary; a Ju Gi-cheol memorial opened in May 2025 (asiae.co.kr).; grade CR
- **Overlap:** IX.2 carries 1907 Pyongyang and ends 1910. IX.11 carries Yoido Full Gospel (1958). IX.16 carries the Unification Church (beyond the floor). IX.24 carries Lausanne. None holds the church family itself.
- **Recommendation:** enter
- **Gate question:** How should the Korean Protestant century enter?
  - (a) One church-family row, 1907-c. 2010, carrying the March First Movement, the shrine crisis, the post-war Presbyterian divisions, minjung theology (as a named line under the scope rule) and the growth decades; Yoido stays in IX.11.
  - (b) One 'Korean Christianity in the twentieth century' row holding Protestants and Catholics together (folds GS-A5).
  - (c) Named lines only, in IX.2 and IX.11.
  - **Recommended:** (a). The Protestant and Catholic stories have separate carriers, dates and texts; (c) repeats the silence the sweep exists to end.

##### 2. GS-A2 — The united churches of South Asia: the Church of South India (1947) and the Church of North India (1970)

- **Kind:** church bodies (two united churches, one row as a family; the Church of Pakistan, 1970, as a named line)
- **Dates:** 1919/1947-c. 2010. Tranquebar meeting May 1919 begins the movement; inauguration 27 September 1947 at St George's Cathedral, Madras; CNI 29 November 1970, Nagpur.
- **Region / countries:** Greater Asia — India, Sri Lanka (Jaffna diocese) [S], Pakistan (Church of Pakistan, named line), Bangladesh [S]
- **Lane:** 6 Protestant & Evangelical (Anglican, Methodist, Reformed and Congregational in one episcopal union)
- **Section A:** Clears on a dated statement, to be opened. The CSI Basis of Union names the Apostles' Creed and the Nicene Creed as 'witnessing to and safeguarding' the faith; that wording appears in a 1950s presentation of the 1951 constitution held at archive.org (ldpd_10984678_000) seen only as a search snippet. Grade: likely clears; the text must be opened before the row is called cleared. CNI: Plan of Church Union, 4th ed. 1965 [S], not seen.
- **B1:** Strong for the CSI: Sundkler 1954 (verified) is the standard history of the union movement; Columbia's Burke Library exhibition documents the inauguration from its archives (verified). Frykenberg 2008 (verified) for the wider frame. CNI thinner: church sources only in this run.
- **B2:** Good. Liturgy (the CSI liturgy), episcopacy accepted by non-episcopal churches, Dalit majority congregations, Tamil and Telugu vernaculars, mission schools and hospitals.
- **B3:** High. The first union anywhere of episcopal and non-episcopal churches, widely read as the ecumenical movement's landmark. The roster names it only inside IX.32 (Newbigin) and in IX.52's relations line.
- **B4:** Thoughtful believers and pastors (what union costs and gives), academic users, historically interested learners.
- **B5:** Large; tiebreak only.
- **Tier:** Tier 1
- **Evidence:**
  - Bengt Sundkler, Church of South India: The Movement Towards Union, 1900-1947 (London: Lutterworth, 1954; Greenwich, CT: Seabury, 1954) — *scholarly study* — **verified** — <https://cambridge.org/core/journals/church-history/article/church-of-south-india-the-movement-towards-union-19001947-by-bengt-sundkler-greenwich-connecticut-the-seabury-press-1954-457-pp-675/EB2DBE1235D7313313EF816FB7CF708D>
  - 'A Church is Born: Church of South India Inauguration', Burke Library exhibition, Columbia University Libraries (archival photographs and papers of 27 Sept 1947; Tranquebar 1919: 33 leaders, 31 Indian) — *archival record of the founding* — **verified** — <https://exhibitions.library.columbia.edu/exhibits/show/church/inauguratingchurch>
  - Tranquebar Manifesto, May 1919 ('We aim not at compromise for the sake of peace, but at the comprehension for the sake of truth') — *founding text* — **[S]** — Line quoted in the Church Times (2017) and paraphrased by Sundkler pp. 101-103 per a Churchman citation; the manifesto itself not opened. Participant count 33 vs 50 disputed.
  - The Constitution of the Church of South India, with amendments up to 31st December 1951 (Basis of Union, creeds clause) — *dated statement of faith* — **[S]** — <https://archive.org/download/ldpd_10984678_000/ldpd_10984678_000.pdf> — Located but not opened in this run.
  - Robert Eric Frykenberg, Christianity in India: From Beginnings to the Present (Oxford: OUP, 2008), Oxford History of the Christian Church — *scholarly survey* — **verified** — <https://muse.jhu.edu/journals/catholic_historical_review/v097/97.4.semple.pdf>
  - Church of North India: inauguration 29 Nov 1970, Nagpur; Plan of Church Union 4th ed. 1965; Methodist Church in Southern Asia stayed out — *dated events* — **[S]** — <https://www.dioceseofdelhi.org/about> — Church sources only.
  - தென்னிந்தியத் திருச்சபை (Tamil: 'Church of South India') own-language records; CSI Tamil liturgy — *own-language founding texts* — **wanted** — Not found in this run. No Tamil, Telugu, Malayalam or Hindi source was verified for this candidate.
- **Quotes (original, then rendering):**
  - (English (original language of the manifesto)) “We aim not at compromise for the sake of peace, but at the comprehension for the sake of truth.” — Tranquebar Manifesto 1919, as quoted by the Church Times 2017 — **[S]**
- **Living today:** True; newest dated evidence: 2020-11-29; CSI 70th-anniversary order of worship, Sept 2017; CNI 50th anniversary, 29 Nov 2020 (nalloorlibrary.com; dioceseofdelhi.org); grade CR
- **Overlap:** IX.32 names Newbigin and the CSI as lineage; IX.52 names the CSI in its relations line; IX.46 holds the WCC. None holds the united churches as a subject.
- **Recommendation:** enter (after the 1951 constitution text is opened for Section A)
- **Gate question:** How should South Asia's united churches enter?
  - (a) One row for the CSI and CNI together (with the Church of Pakistan as a named line), 1919/1947-c. 2010, opening on the Dalit mass movements as its people (GS-A3 folded as a named line).
  - (b) The CSI alone as a row; the CNI as a named line.
  - (c) A named line inside IX.46 (the ecumenical movement).
  - **Recommended:** (a). The two unions are one negotiated tradition (1919-1970), and (c) would make the subject of the story a footnote to Geneva.

##### 3. GS-A3 — The Dalit and Adivasi mass movements into the churches

- **Kind:** cross-church movement (group conversion into several Protestant churches and the Catholic Church)
- **Dates:** c. 1870s-c. 1940 (era-9 roots; its documented, named phase falls in era 10). Contemporaries already called them 'mass movements'; Pickett's National Christian Council survey (1933) named and measured them.
- **Region / countries:** Greater Asia — India, Pakistan (Punjab)
- **Lane:** 6 Protestant & Evangelical (with a Catholic wing)
- **Section A:** Not a confession of its own. The people entered churches that clear the floor (Methodist, Anglican, Lutheran, Baptist, Catholic); assessed at the world level per A5.
- **B1:** Good. Pickett 1933 (verified) is a contemporary study with its own data; Harper 2000 (verified) on Azariah and the Dornakal mass movement; Frykenberg 2008. The people's own voices are thin in print: the asymmetry Article 20 names.
- **B2:** Good: caste, land, schooling, village worship, the colonial census, the Gandhi-Azariah conversion debate of the 1930s.
- **B3:** High. Most Indian Protestants descend from these movements; the roster has no Dalit subject at all.
- **B4:** Historically interested learners, academic users, deconstructing Christians (conversion and power), pastors.
- **B5:** Large.
- **Tier:** Tier 2
- **Four marks:** beginning — Group movements among Telugu Madigas and Malas and Punjabi Chuhras from the 1870s [S, Frykenberg]; their twentieth-century peak in Dornakal under V. S. Azariah, bishop from 1912 (Harper 2000).; carriers — Village catechists and converts' own kin networks; Azariah's diocese; Methodist and Anglican missions.; name — 'Mass movements' — the term in Pickett's 1933 title.; recognition — Pickett 1933 (contemporary NCC study); Harper 2000; Frykenberg 2008.
- **Evidence:**
  - J. Waskom Pickett, Christian Mass Movements in India: A Study with Recommendations (New York: Abingdon, 1933) — *contemporary study / dated founding record of the name* — **verified** — <https://relbib.de/Record/1097634779/Description>
  - Susan Billington Harper, In the Shadow of the Mahatma: Bishop V. S. Azariah and the Travails of Christianity in British India (Grand Rapids: Eerdmans, 2000) — *scholarly study* — **verified** — <https://cambridge.org/core/journals/church-history/article/in-the-shadow-of-the-mahatma-bishop-v-s-azariah-and-the-travails-of-christianity-in-british-india-by-harper-susan-billington-studies-in-the-history-of-christian-missions-grand-rapids-mich-eerdmans-2000-xxii-462-pp-4500-cloth/2A063A88C8C4270201EFD19F252D430E>
  - Frykenberg 2008 (as GS-A2) — *scholarly survey* — **verified**
  - Telugu, Punjabi or Hindi own-voice records of the movements — *own-language sources* — **wanted** — None found in this run.
- **Living today:** n/a (a historical movement; its churches live on in GS-A2); newest dated evidence: None; None; grade None
- **Overlap:** None in era 10. VIII.7 (missionary native churches) names Serampore India but not the mass movements.
- **Recommendation:** named line in GS-A2 (opening its story), unless Mark wants a Dalit subject as its own row

  - (b) Their own movement row, c. 1870s-c. 1940, spanning eras 9 and 10.
  - (c) Hold.
  - **Recommended:** (a), because their decisive history is the people the united churches were made of. (b) is defensible on the four marks and is the better choice if GS-A2 is declined, but it is not yet eligible: the evidence rule also needs the movement's own dated texts, and those are wanted.

##### 4. GS-A4 — Chinese Catholicism: from the first Chinese bishops to the divided church

- **Kind:** church body (the Catholic Church in China, open and underground communities together)
- **Dates:** 1906-c. 2010. Key dated points: Primum Concilium Sinense, Shanghai, 15 May-12/13 June 1924; six Chinese bishops consecrated by Pius XI, 28 Oct 1926; the rites ruling of 8 Dec 1939; hierarchy erected by Quotidie Nos, 11 Apr 1946; Catholic Patriotic Association, July-Aug 1957; first bishops consecrated without papal mandate, 13 Apr 1958; Ad Apostolorum Principis, 29 June 1958.
- **Region / countries:** Greater Asia — China
- **Lane:** 5 Latin West & Catholicism
- **Section A:** Clears: the Catholic Church's creed (Nicene). The divided communities share it; the division is jurisdictional, not confessional.
- **B1:** Good. Madsen 1998 (UC Press, verified) is the standard study; the Holy Spirit Study Centre, Hong Kong, hosts Quotidie Nos (verified link). Chinese-language primary texts of 1957-58 located only through a 2025 article not opened.
- **B2:** Strong: whole Catholic villages, lineage and rites, martyrdom and imprisonment, a split hierarchy, rural-urban differences (Madsen).
- **B3:** High. IX.5 'The Chinese Church' is wholly Protestant (zero mentions of Catholics) and sits in the Pentecostal lane; China's Catholics have no row in any era after era 7/8.
- **B4:** Historically interested learners, Catholics, academic users; the open/underground question is one participants ask.
- **B5:** Large (c. 3 million at 1949 to about 10 million by the late 1990s, per Madsen's reviewers; estimates).
- **Tier:** Tier 1
- **Evidence:**
  - Richard Madsen, China's Catholics: Tragedy and Hope in an Emerging Civil Society (Berkeley: University of California Press, 1998) — *scholarly study* — **verified** — <https://cambridge.org/core/journals/church-history/article/abs/chinas-catholics-tragedy-and-hope-in-an-emerging-civil-society-by-richard-madsen-comparative-studies-in-religion-and-society-12-berkeleyuniversity-of-california-press-1998-xiv-186-pp-2750-cloth/527365D169D97948CB4E4283E76EAF01>
  - Pius XII, Quotidie Nos, 11 April 1946 (erects the Chinese hierarchy) — *dated founding text* — **verified** — <https://hsstudyc.org.hk/oldwebsite/en/china/chdoc/19460411.pdf>
  - Primum Concilium Sinense, Shanghai 1924 (Latin acts); six bishops 28 Oct 1926 — *dated events* — **[S]** — Dates from Catholic news sources (Catholic World Report 2024; Fides); closing date 12 or 13 June conflicts.
  - 中国天主教爱国会 (Chinese Catholic Patriotic Association), founding conference Beijing, July-August 1957; 自选自圣 ('self-election, self-consecration') from 13 April 1958 — *dated events, Chinese terms* — **[S]** — From a search summary of an MDPI Religions article (2025, vol. 16 no. 9, art. 1138), which returned 403. Its Chinese primary sources not seen.
  - Pius XII, Ad Apostolorum Principis, 29 June 1958 — *dated text* — **verified** — <https://www.vatican.va/content/pius-xii/en/encyclicals/documents/hf_p-xii_enc_29061958_ad-apostolorum-principis.html> — The search summary gave 29 July; the encyclical's own page gives 29 June 1958.
- **Living today:** True; newest dated evidence: 2026-11-05; Leo XIV video message to a conference on China, 5 Nov 2026 (vatican.va) and the 2026 centenary of the six bishops (chinaaid.org report); grade CR
- **Overlap:** IX.5 (Protestant only); IX.47 (Rome-centered, no China line). VII/VIII-era China rows not checked for Catholic content past 1906.
- **Recommendation:** enter
- **Gate question:** How should China's Catholics enter?
  - (a) Their own row, 1906-c. 2010.
  - (b) Widen IX.5 into 'Chinese Christianity' with a Catholic wing (and reconsider its Pentecostal lane).
  - (c) A named line in IX.47.
  - **Recommended:** (a). IX.5 is built on Protestant founding texts; folding Catholics in would double its subject and leave the lane wrong for both.

##### 11. GS-A11 — Pacific Island churches in the twentieth century: self-government, the Pacific Conference of Churches and the Melanesian Brotherhood

- **Kind:** church family (the island churches after the missions) with one defined movement inside it
- **Dates:** 1925-c. 2010. Melanesian Brotherhood founded 28 Oct 1925 by Ini Kopuria; first Pacific Conference of Churches and Missions, Malua [S], Samoa, 1961; PCC constituted at Lifou, 25 May-7 June 1966; Pacific Theological College, Suva, 1966.
- **Region / countries:** Pacific — Fiji, Samoa, Tonga, Solomon Islands, Vanuatu, Papua New Guinea, New Caledonia
- **Lane:** 6 Protestant & Evangelical (Methodist, Congregational, Anglican, Presbyterian; Catholics as a named line)
- **Section A:** Not yet assessable as one row: the member churches' own statements were not seen. The Melanesian Brotherhood is Anglican and clears through the Anglican formularies (A5).
- **B1:** Adequate. Garrett 1997 (verified; IPS-USP with WCC), the third of a three-volume regional history; Macdonald-Milne 2003 (verified) on the Brotherhood; Project Canterbury holds Bishop Steward's 1928 account of the Brothers (located). Pacific-language sources (Samoan, Tongan, Fijian, Mota) not readable or verifiable in this run.
- **B2:** Strong: chiefly order and church, village pastors, the Brotherhood's vows and peace-making, independence-era churches.
- **B3:** High. The roster's Pacific is VIII.7's island teachers (to 1906) and IX.26's Ratana and adjustment movements (register); the mainline island churches that became national churches have no row.
- **B4:** Historically interested learners, Pacific diaspora, academic users.
- **B5:** Small to mid; very high share of population.
- **Tier:** Tier 2
- **Four marks:** beginning — Melanesian Brotherhood: 28 Oct 1925, Tabalia, Guadalcanal.; carriers — Ini Kopuria and seven first brothers; Bishop John Steward.; name — Ira Retatasiu ('the Company of Brothers', Mota) [S via Solomon Encyclopaedia].; recognition — Macdonald-Milne 2003; Garrett 1997.
- **Evidence:**
  - John Garrett, Where Nets Were Cast: Christianity in Oceania since World War II (Suva and Geneva: Institute of Pacific Studies, USP, with WCC, 1997) — *scholarly study* — **verified** — <https://lir.byuh.edu/index.php/pacific/article/view/315>
  - Brian Macdonald-Milne, The True Way of Service: The Pacific Story of the Melanesian Brotherhood, 1925-2000 (Leicester: Christians Aware, 2003) — *scholarly/insider history* — **verified** — <https://archivesearch.lib.cam.ac.uk/repositories/24/archival_objects/500656> — Cambridge University Library archive record ("The True Way of Service – The Pacific Story of the Melanesian Brotherhood 1925-2000 by Brian Macdonald-Milne, 2003"); Christians Aware, Leicester, 2003, confirmed by booksellers' records (Round 1). The earlier link was an encyclopedia page.
  - J. M. Steward, account of the Melanesian Brothers (1928), Project Canterbury — *dated founding text* — **[S]** — <https://anglicanhistory.org/oceania/jmsteward/brothers1928/> — Located; not opened.
  - Pacific Conference of Churches, own history (formed 1961; inaugural Assembly at Lifou, 1966) — *own-voice* — **verified** — <https://www.pcc.org.fj/?p=6> — The page does not name Malua; Malua is [S].
- **Living today:** True; newest dated evidence: not fixed in this run; PCC website (current); Melanesian Brotherhood page of the Anglican Religious Life Year Book (both the body's own word, undated); grade none (no dated item after 2010)
- **Overlap:** VIII.7 (island teachers, to 1906); IX.26 (Ratana; Christian Fellowship Church); IX.46 (PCC is a WCC-linked council); IX.52 (Anglicans).
- **Recommendation:** enter once Section A is settled on dated statements of the member churches, as one row; Melanesian Brotherhood as its named movement line
- **Gate question:** How should the island churches enter?
  - (a) One row, 1925-c. 2010, with the Brotherhood and the PCC as named lines.
  - (b) The Melanesian Brotherhood alone as a movement row.
  - (c) Named lines in IX.26 and IX.52.
  - **Recommended:** (a). IX.26 is a register row for movements that left the mission churches; putting the mainline churches inside it would reverse its meaning.

## 6. Eastern Europe and the former Soviet world and the Middle East

The region's `candidates.md`, as written, with its headings demoted two levels.

### Era 10 global completeness sweep — Eastern Europe, the former Soviet world and the Middle East (1906–c. 2010)

Researcher: Opus, gs-east. Branch `claude/era10-global-sweep`, read only. Census read: `cic-website/data/world-census.json` (release on the branch, 56 era-10 rows). No census copy is drafted here (teaser/why/longDescription wait for Mark's gate). No metered model calls.

**Verification states used.** **V** = bibliographic detail or date confirmed in this run, URL given. **V-q** = quotation read verbatim in this run, URL or Library file given. **V-t** = confirmed only from a tertiary or encyclopedic page (Wikipedia, OrthodoxWiki, Britannica); usable as a pointer, not as a scholarly anchor. **V-s** = seen only in a search-result snippet, page not opened. **[S]** = not verified in this run. An [S] or V-s item never counts as verified.

**Non-English sources.** Searched in Russian, Ukrainian, Polish, Romanian, Serbian, Bulgarian, Greek, Arabic and Lithuanian. Every item is cited in its original-language title with an English gloss. Quotes are given in the original with a close English rendering marked *(my rendering)*. Armenian, Syriac and Georgian searches were not run: their rivers already have rows (IX.27, IX.19, IX.40) and no gap candidate in those rivers came up (see §1, notes).

**The Library (`cic/texts/REGISTRY.yaml`, 1,531 files).** The region's non-English holdings are rich but almost wholly pre-1906: Russian Old Believer and synodal editions (Subbotin, Avvakum, *Dukhovnyi reglament*), Ruthenian/Polish union polemic (Smotrycki, Skarga), Church Slavonic (Stoglav, Nil Sorsky, Paisius), Syriac and Arabic (Synodicon Orientale, Duwayhi's Maronite history, Chronicle of Seert), Armenian (Ormanian 1912). **No vendored file is an era-10 primary source for this region.** Two in-era items exist and are scholarship, not own voice:
- `conybeare_russian-dissenters_1921.txt` (Harvard Theological Studies X, 1921) — V-q. Preface (file lines 102–105, OCR double spaces normalized): "This work was begun in 1914 and completed nearly as it stands early in 1917, about the time when the Russian Revolution began. It is too early yet to trace the fortunes of the Russian sects during this latest period". Its chapter on "The Stundists" is a near-contemporary witness for candidate E4's roots to 1917.
- `likowski_unia-brzeska-pol_1907.txt` — *Unia brzeska (r. 1596)* ("The Union of Brest of 1596"), Warsaw 1907, Polish — V. Printed inside the era, but its subject is 1596; no use as era-10 evidence.
