VIII.40 holds the Chaldean consolidation to 1906. | The Maronite Church in Greater Lebanon (1920), the civil war, the Patriarchal Synod (2003–2006), the 2010 Synod for the Middle East; the Chaldean church in modern Iraq (Delly 2003; Rahho 2008). | **E6** The Maronite Church — Greater Lebanon to the Patriarchal Synod (1920–c. 2010); **E10** Chaldean line in IX.19 |
| **Holy Land / Palestinian** | IX.13 Messianic Judaism (Israel). Nothing for Arab Christians of Palestine/Israel. | The churches of the Holy Land after 1948; Palestinian liberation theology (Ateek 1989, Sabeel 1991); Kairos Palestine (11 December 2009). | **E7** Palestinian Christians — from the Nakba to Kairos (1948–c. 2010) |
| **Protestant & Evangelical — Soviet/East European** | VIII.51 Russian Evangelicals to 1906; IX.12 Initsiativniki (1961–) only. | The registered body and its history: Prokhanov's Evangelical Christians, the Baptist Union, the 1944 AUCECB merger, the 1985 confession, the post-Soviet growth (Wanner 2007). | **E4** Soviet and Post-Soviet Evangelicals and Pentecostals (1905–c. 2010) |
| **Protestant — ecumenical, East-Central Europe** | IX.46 WCC (Geneva). | The Christian Peace Conference (Prague, 1958). The GDR "church in socialism" and the 1989 prayer meetings (not researched; North Europe sweep). | **E11** Christian Peace Conference — named line in IX.46 |
| **Pentecostal & charismatic** | IX.11 (Global South), IX.1, IX.45 — none in this region. | Soviet Pentecostals (Voronaev; the 1945 merger; the 1978–83 embassy refugees [S]); Romanian Pentecostal union (1922, recognized 1950). | Named lines in **E4** |
| **Independent / initiated** | — | No region-born independent church body of row weight found. The Old Calendarist churches (1924/1935) are the nearest, and are Orthodox. Old Believers in the 20th century have no receiver after VII.7 (era 8) — not researched for era 10. | Old Calendarists as named line in **E3b**; Old Believers held |
| **Syriac / Church of the East** | IX.19 (both wings), IX.39 (India). | Chaldean side of VIII.40 has no era-10 home beyond IX.19's "Assyro-Chaldean delegation". | **E10** named line in IX.19 |

---

#### 2. Candidates, ranked by importance to the era's global story

##### E1 — The Russian Orthodox Church — Council, Patriarchate and Revival (1917–c. 2010)

- **Kind:** church body (church row). Successor of VIII.31 (Synodal Century, ends 1906).
- **Dates:** 1917 (Local Council opens 28 August 1917) – c. 2010. Living.
- **Region / countries:** North Europe (census region used for Russia). Russia; with Belarus, Ukraine, Moldova, the Baltic states and Central Asia as canonical territory [S for the list].
- **Lane:** 3 Greek East & Orthodoxy.
- **What it carries that no row does:** the 1917–18 Council and the restored patriarchate as an institution (IX.22 already narrates Tikhon, including his 1918 anathema; see Overlap); the 1943 settlement and the 1945 Council; the 1958–64 Khrushchev campaign; the 1971 Council; the 1988 Millennium Council; the 1988–2010 revival of parishes and monasteries; the Basic Social Concept (2000); the Act of Canonical Communion with the Church Abroad (2007), as an act of the church (IX.22 cites the Act and the August 2000 Jubilee Council inside its Butovo story; see Overlap); Kirill's election (2009).
- **Section A:** No floor question. The church confesses the Nicene-Constantinopolitan Creed, the same body assessed at VIII.31. Dated own statements inside the era: the Council's definitions of 1917–18; *Основы социальной концепции Русской Православной Церкви* (2000), whose section I sets out its theological premises (V, azbyka). A3: none.
- **B1 Sourcing:** Strong. Own-voice texts are dated and published (Council acts; 2000 doctrine; 2007 Act). Standard histories in English and Russian, from archives opened after 1991.
- **B2 Ecology:** Strong: parish, monastery, council, state archive, émigré, dissident (Yakunin [S]), laity.
- **B3 Uniqueness:** High. The roster tells this church only through its martyrs (IX.22) and émigrés (IX.9). The institution that survived, bargained, divided and revived is not on the tree.
- **B4 User needs:** Thoughtful believers, pastors, history learners, academically minded users; Orthodox inquirers.
- **B5 Scale:** The largest Orthodox church; modest tiebreak only.
- **Tier:** 1.
- **Evidence:**
  1. *Собрание определений и постановлений Священного Собора Православной Российской Церкви 1917–1918 гг.* ("Collected definitions and resolutions of the Holy Council of the Orthodox Russian Church, 1917–1918"), Moscow 1918, vols 1–2, appendix to the *Деяния* ("Acts") — online at azbyka.ru — **V-q**. Definition "О высшем управлении Православной Российской Церкви" ("On the higher administration of the Orthodox Russian Church"), 4 November 1917: «Восстановляется Патриаршество, и управление церковное возглавляется Патриархом.» — "The Patriarchate is restored, and the church's government is headed by the Patriarch." *(my rendering)* <https://azbyka.ru/otechnik/Istorija_Tserkvi/opredelenija-sobora-pravoslavnoj-rossijskoj-tserkvi-1917-1918-gg/>
  2. Election of Tikhon, 5 November 1917 (Old Style); vote of 28 October to proceed on restoration — V-s (mospat.ru, azbyka "Выборы Патриарха в Русской Церкви в XX веке"). <https://azbyka.ru/vybory-patriarxa-v-russkoj-cerkvi-v-xx-veke>
  3. Поместный Собор 1988 г. ("The Local Council of 1988"), Trinity-Sergius Lavra, 6–9 June 1988, for the Millennium of the Baptism of Rus' — V-t. <https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1988_Local_Council_of_the_Russian_Orthodox_Church>
  4. *Основы социальной концепции Русской Православной Церкви* ("The Basis of the Social Concept of the Russian Orthodox Church"), adopted by the Bishops' Council, 13–16 August 2000 — V. <https://azbyka.ru/osnovy-socialnoj-koncepcii-russkoj-pravoslavnoj-cerkvi>
  5. *Акт о каноническом общении* ("Act of Canonical Communion"), Moscow, 4/17 May 2007, signed by Patriarch Alexy II and Metropolitan Laurus — V (date and signatories from the azbyka text page as surfaced). <https://azbyka.ru/otechnik/dokumenty/akt-o-kanonicheskom-obshhenii/>
  6. Dimitry Pospielovsky, *The Russian Church under the Soviet Regime, 1917–1982*, 2 vols (Crestwood, NY: St Vladimir's Seminary Press, 1984) — V. <https://blackwells.co.uk/bookshop/product/The-Russian-Church-Under-the-Soviet-Regime-1917-1982-by-Dimitry-Pospielovsky/9780881410150>
  7. Nathaniel Davis, *A Long Walk to Church: A Contemporary History of Russian Orthodoxy* (Boulder: Westview, 1995; 2nd ed. 2003, which adds the post-communist period) — V. <https://www.routledge.com/A-Long-Walk-To-Church-A-Contemporary-History-Of-Russian-Orthodoxy/Davis/p/book/9780813340678>
  8. М. В. Шкаровский, *Русская Православная Церковь при Сталине и Хрущеве (Государственно-церковные отношения в СССР в 1939–1964 годах)* ("The Russian Orthodox Church under Stalin and Khrushchev: church-state relations in the USSR, 1939–1964"), Moscow: Krutitsy Patriarchal Metochion, 1999 — V. <https://www.azbyka.ru/otechnik/Istorija_Tserkvi/russkaja-pravoslavnaja-tserkov-pri-staline-i-hrushheve-gosudarstvenno-tserkovnye-otnoshenija-v-sssr-v-1939-1964-godah/>
  9. Tatiana A. Chumachenko, *Church and State in Soviet Russia: Russian Orthodoxy from World War II to the Khrushchev Years*, ed. and tr. Edward E. Roslof (Armonk, NY: M. E. Sharpe, 2002); first published in Russian as *Государство, православная церковь, верующие. 1941–1961 гг.* ("State, Orthodox Church, believers, 1941–1961"), Moscow 1999 — V (English); V-s (Russian title from the JEH review's catalog line). <https://libcat.library.tamu.edu/Record/in00001713332>
  10. Lucian N. Leustean (ed.), *Eastern Christianity and the Cold War, 1945–91* (London: Routledge, 2010) — V. <https://www.routledge.com/Eastern-Christianity-and-the-Cold-War-1945-91/Leustean/p/book/9780415471978>
  11. Vera Shevzov — named in the brief; no title verified in this run [S].
- **Living:** yes. Newest dated evidence gathered: the 2007 Act and 2009 election are in-era; no dated present-day own-voice page was opened. Grade **CR**, uncertain until a dated mospat.ru item is read.
- **Overlap:** IX.22 keeps persecution, the 1927 Declaration and the catacomb split, and the 2000 canonization. IX.9 keeps the emigration. IX.48 keeps the OCA's 1970 autocephaly. E1 must name each and not retell them. Shared with IX.22: its census text narrates Tikhon (including his 1918 anathema) and cites the Act of Canonical Communion of 17 May 2007 and the August 2000 Jubilee Council as primary sources, inside its Butovo story. The proposed split: IX.22 tells Tikhon's story and the 2000 canonization; E1 tells the Council's acts and the restored patriarchate as an institution, the 2000 Council's social doctrine, and the 2007 Act as the reunion of the church. Each names the other. VIII.11's Optina line already runs through IX.22.
- **Recommendation:** **enter** (Tier 1 church row).
- **Gate question G-E1.** How should the Russian church's institutional story enter era 10?
  - (a) A new church row, 1917–c. 2010, successor of VIII.31, with IX.22 and IX.9 as its named kin. **Recommended**: it closes the V2.0 D-P2 gap on sources that are now verified, and keeps IX.22 a persecution row.
  - (b) Extend IX.22 to c. 2010 and widen it to the whole church. This blurs a defined martyrdom row.
  - (c) Keep D-P2's "named gap, no row".

##### E2 — Polish Catholicism — Wyszyński, John Paul II and Solidarity (1918–c. 2010)

- **Kind:** church body (the Catholic Church in Poland, a national church within the Catholic communion).
- **Dates:** 1918 (independence) – c. 2010. Living.
- **Region / countries:** North Europe (the tag VI.20, VII.28 and VIII.38 use for Polish-Lithuanian lands); Poland.
- **Lane:** 5 Latin West & Catholicism.
- **Section A:** No floor question (Catholic; the Creed).
- **B1:** Strong. Dated episcopal letters are published in Polish (*Listy pasterskie Episkopatu Polski 1945–2000*, Marki: Michalineum, 2003 — V-s); the IPN (Institute of National Remembrance) has published state files; standard histories in English and Polish.
- **B2:** Strong: pilgrimage (Jasna Góra), parish, catechesis, youth movement, worker movement, state security files, the papal visits.
- **B3:** High. No Eastern European Catholic row exists; the era's most consequential church-state story in Europe after 1945 is absent.
- **B4:** History learners, Catholics, academically minded users; the John Paul II question every participant will ask.
- **B5:** Large.
- **Tier:** 1.
- **Named lines inside the row:** the Light-Life movement (Ruch Światło-Życie): beginning 1954 (first oasis retreat), carriers Fr Franciszek Blachnicki, name "Ruch Żywego Kościoła" 1969–76 then "Ruch Światło-Życie" from 1976, recognized in a monograph (Derewenda 2010). Three of the four marks are verified; recognition (Derewenda 2010) is V-s; it is a named line, not a row, because its story sits wholly inside this church. Popiełuszko (killed October 1984; exact date [S]).
- **Evidence:**
  1. *Jasnogórskie Śluby Narodu Polskiego* ("The Jasna Góra Vows of the Polish Nation"), written by Cardinal Wyszyński while interned at Komańcza (completed 16 May 1956), read at Jasna Góra on 26 August 1956 by Bishop Michał Klepacz — V (dzieje.pl; IPN). <https://dzieje.pl/node/169989>. Text not opened; quote not taken.
  2. *Orędzie biskupów polskich do ich niemieckich braci w Chrystusowym urzędzie pasterskim* ("Message of the Polish bishops to their German brothers in Christ's pastoral office"), Rome, 18 November 1965, drafted by Abp Bolesław Kominek, signed by 34 bishops incl. Wyszyński and Wojtyła — V (dzieje.pl). Its famous line «Przebaczamy i prosimy o przebaczenie» — "We forgive and we ask for forgiveness" *(my rendering)* — **V-s only** (snippet; text not opened). <https://dzieje.pl/node/22372>
  3. John Paul II, homily, Plac Zwycięstwa, Warsaw, 2 June 1979: «Niech zstąpi Duch Twój i odnowi oblicze ziemi». «Tej ziemi!» — "Let your Spirit come down and renew the face of the earth." "Of this land!" *(my rendering)* — **V-q**, as quoted on vatican.va in Benedict XVI's Warsaw homily of 26 May 2006; the 1979 text itself not opened. <https://www.vatican.va/content/benedict-xvi/pl/homilies/2006/documents/hf_ben-xvi_hom_20060526_varsavia.html>
  4. Jerzy Kłoczowski, *A History of Polish Christianity* (Cambridge: CUP, 2000), tr. of *Dzieje chrześcijaństwa polskiego* ("History of Polish Christianity"), 2 vols (Paris: Éditions du Dialogue, 1987, 1991) — V. <https://assets.cambridge.org/97805213/64294/copyright/9780521364294_copyright_info.pdf>
  5. Brian Porter-Szűcs, *Faith and Fatherland: Catholicism, Modernity, and Poland* (New York: OUP, 2011) — V (later scholarship on in-era matters; allowed). <https://cambridge.org/core/journals/church-history/article/abs/faith-and-fatherland-catholicism-modernity-and-poland-by-brian-porterszucs-new-york-oxford-university-press-2011-ix-484-pp-5500-cloth/7E94AB1C719DE11A65B2AC4C586C22D3>
  6. George Weigel, *The Final Revolution: The Resistance Church and the Collapse of Communism* (New York: OUP, 1992) — V (publisher page); advocacy-positioned, reviewers divided. <https://eppc.org/book/the-final-revolution-the-resistance-church-and-the-collapse-of-communism>
  7. Robert Derewenda, *Dzieło wiary. Historia Ruchu Światło-Życie w latach 1950–1985* ("A work of faith: history of the Light-Life Movement, 1950–1985"), Kraków 2010 — V-s (cited in an IPN journal article). IPN brochure on Blachnicki — V. <https://lublin.ipn.gov.pl/download/210/840250/BroszuraFranciszekBlachnickim.pdf>
- **Living:** yes. Newest dated item seen: Vatican News (Polish), August 2026, Abp Wojda on the 70th anniversary of the vows — V-s, **CR**.
- **Overlap:** IX.47/IX.23 (Rome), IX.7 (lay movements — Light-Life is not in it). VI.20/VII.28/VIII.38 are the Greek Catholic line, not this one.
- **Recommendation:** **enter** (Tier 1 church row), Light-Life and Popiełuszko as named lines.
- **Gate question G-E2.** How should East-Central European Catholicism enter?
  - (a) A Polish church row, 1918–c. 2010, with Lithuania as a named line (E12). **Recommended**: the Polish sources are verified now; the rest of the bloc is not.
  - (b) One composite row, "The Catholic Church under communism in East-Central Europe" (Poland, Lithuania, Czechoslovakia, Hungary, Croatia), 1945–1989. Wider, but four of its five legs are [S] today, and it cuts Poland's 1918–45 and 1989–2010.
  - (c) A Polish row now, and a composite row for the rest held for sources.

##### E3a — Orthodoxy in the Communist Balkans — the Serbian, Romanian, Bulgarian and Albanian Churches (1918–c. 2010)

- **Kind:** church-family row (as VIII.28 is). Successor of VIII.28 for the Slavic and Romanian churches.
- **Dates:** 1918/1920 – c. 2010. Living.
- **Region / countries:** Asia Minor and Mediterranean, as VIII.28 and VII.18 use; Serbia, Romania, Bulgaria, Albania, North Macedonia [S], Montenegro [S].
- **Lane:** 3 Greek East & Orthodoxy.
- **Section A:** No floor question.
- **B1:** Adequate-to-strong. Dates verified for each patriarchate; Leustean (ed.) 2010 and Ramet (ed.) 1988 are standard; national-language archives and church publications. Per-church own-voice texts not opened in this run.
- **B2:** Strong: patriarchates, monasteries, the communist cults ministries, the Romanian "Burning Bush" monks, Securitate files, Albania's total ban, the post-1989 return.
- **B3:** High. VIII.28's national churches vanish at 1906. IX.12 holds one Romanian renewal movement, not the churches.
- **B4:** History learners, Orthodox inquirers, academically minded users.
- **B5:** Romania alone is the second-largest Orthodox church [S].
- **Tier:** 1 (as a family row; each national leg is Tier 2 on its own).
- **Named lines:** the Burning Bush (Rugul Aprins), Antim monastery, Bucharest, from 1945 (some sources 1944), outlawed 1948, members arrested June 1958 and tried November 1958 — V (Stanciu 2025; Cluj thesis). The four marks are met (beginning 1945; carriers Sandu Tudor/Fr Daniil, Benedict Ghiuș, Sofian Boghiu; name "Rugul Aprins"; studied as a movement). The only own-voice text seen is a 1999 posthumous edition (item 8, V-s). It ends in 1958. A line, not a row: its story sits inside the Romanian church. IX.12's Lord's Army stays where it is.
- **Evidence:**
  1. Romanian Patriarchate: Holy Synod decision 4 February 1925; law of 25 February 1925; Constantinople's Tomos of 30 July 1925; Miron Cristea enthroned 1 November 1925 — V (patriarhia.ro). <https://patriarhia.ro/en/history-of-the-romanian-orthodox-church/patriarchs-of-romania/patriarch-miron-cristea/>
  2. Serbian Patriarchate: unification proclaimed at Sremski Karlovci, 12 September 1920; Constantinople's Tomos no. 1149, 19 February 1922; Patriarch Dimitrije — V (Новости, «Век од добијања Томоса од Васељенске патријаршије за уједињење СПЦ», "A century since the Tomos from the Ecumenical Patriarchate for the unification of the Serbian Orthodox Church"). Dimitrije's election date conflicts across sources (28 September / 12 November 1920) — Contested at detail level. <https://www.novosti.rs/c/drustvo/vesti/1087855/susret-istorijom-obnova-stare-slave-crkve-vek-dobijanja-tomosa-vaseljenske-patrijarsije-ujedinjenje-spc>
  3. Bulgarian Patriarchate: schism lifted 1945; Third Church-People's Council restores the patriarchate, 10 May 1953, electing Kiril of Plovdiv — V (Bulgarian church statement as reported). Bulgarian search: «Българска патриаршия възстановена 10 май 1953» ("Bulgarian Patriarchate restored 10 May 1953"). <https://orthochristian.com/153534.html>
  4. Albania: autocephaly declared at the Congress of Berat, 17 September 1922 (Fan Noli); Tomos of 1937 (12 or 17 April — sources differ); Anastasios Yannoulatos elected 24 June 1992 — V-t (OrthodoxWiki; CNEWA). The 1967 ban on religion — [S], not confirmed in this run. <https://cnewa.org/eastern-christian-churches/orthodox-church/the-autocephalous-churches/the-orthodox-church-of-albania/>
  5. Lucian N. Leustean (ed.), *Eastern Christianity and the Cold War, 1945–91* (Routledge, 2010) — V (has a Romanian chapter by the editor).
  6. Pedro (Sabrina P.) Ramet (ed.), *Eastern Christianity and Politics in the Twentieth Century* (Durham: Duke UP, 1988) — V. <https://cambridge.org/core/journals/american-political-science-review/article/abs/eastern-christianity-and-politics-in-the-twentieth-century-edited-by-pedro-ramet-durham-duke-university-press-1988-471p-4750/2A186FE97F670C1F445E110AE6FFDB68>
  7. Mihail Stanciu, «Importanța mișcării isihaste „Rugul Aprins" în spiritualitatea ortodoxă și în cultura română» ("The importance of the hesychast movement 'Burning Bush' in Orthodox spirituality and Romanian culture"), *Teologie și Educație* 23 (2025), 77–103 — V. <https://gup.ugal.ro/ugaljournals/index.php/teologie/article/view/9507>; Cluj doctoral thesis «„Rugul Aprins" de la mănăstirea Antim: istorie, memorie, discurs» ("The Burning Bush of Antim monastery: history, memory, discourse") — V-s, author and year [S].
  8. Ieroschimonah Daniil (Sandu Tudor), *Taina Rugului Aprins* ("The mystery of the Burning Bush"), Bucharest: Anastasia, 1999 — V-s (own-voice writings, posthumous edition).
- **Living:** yes (the churches). Dated items seen: Romanian centenary of the first patriarch's enthronement (orthodoxtimes, 2025) and Bulgarian 70th-anniversary statement (2023) — V-s, **CR**. Burning Bush line: living **no** (ended 1958).
- **Overlap:** VIII.28 (predecessor), IX.12 (Lord's Army), IX.27/IX.40 (other national churches under Soviet rule).
- **Recommendation:** **enter** as a family row.
- **Gate question G-E3.** One Balkan row or two?
  - (a) One family row for the Balkan and Greek churches together, as VIII.28 had.
  - (b) Two rows: E3a for the churches under communism (Serbia, Romania, Bulgaria, Albania) and E3b for the Church of Greece and the Ecumenical Patriarchate. **Recommended**: after 1945 the two halves lived under different political orders, and the Greek half's story (Zoe, the calendar schism, the Phanar in Turkey, 1965) does not share the communist half's sources or questions.
  - (c) Four national rows. The verified base does not yet support four rows.

##### E4 — Soviet and Post-Soviet Evangelicals and Pentecostals (1905–c. 2010)

- **Kind:** church-family row (the registered Evangelical Christian–Baptist union and its Pentecostal kin). Successor of VIII.51.
- **Dates:** 1905 (toleration; overlaps VIII.51's end) or 1906 – c. 2010. Living.
- **Region / countries:** North Europe (as VIII.51); Russia, Ukraine, Belarus, Moldova; Romania for the Pentecostal line.
- **Lane:** 6 Protestant & Evangelical (with 7 for the Pentecostal line).
- **Section A:** On a dated statement: «Вероучение евангельских христиан-баптистов» ("The Doctrine of the Evangelical Christians-Baptists"), adopted at the 43rd All-Union Congress, 21–23 March 1985, which the Ukrainian union says continues Pavlov's 1906 and Prokhanov's 1910 confessions — V (date and lineage from the union's own page); text **not read**. So Section A is "on a dated statement, text to be read before entry". No floor question expected (Baptist trinitarian confessions) [S].
- **B1:** Strong. Coleman 2005, Sawatsky 1981 (already repo-cited at IX.12), Savinsky in Russian, Wanner 2007; union and state records.
- **B2:** Strong: congregations, choirs, the 1944 forced merger, registration, dissidence, emigration, post-Soviet missions.
- **B3:** High. VIII.51 stops in 1906, and IX.12 tells only the 1961 dissident wing. The majority body that registered with the state has no row.
- **B4:** Evangelicals, pastors, history learners; deconstructing and seeking users asking about faith under pressure.
- **B5:** The largest Baptist community in Europe in the Soviet period [S].
- **Tier:** 2, on B1–B5. The Protestant river's regional imbalance is the sweep's purpose, not a Section B criterion, so it goes to the gate question (G-125), not the tier.
- **Named lines:** Soviet Pentecostals (Voronaev in Ukraine [S for 1921]; merged into the AUCECB after 1944 under state pressure — V-t); the Romanian Pentecostal union (founded at Păuliș, Arad county, 10 September 1922, by Gheorghe Bradin; recognized by the state 1950; *Buletinul Cultului Penticostal* 1953–89 — V-t / V for the periodical catalog); the post-Soviet missionary wave (CoMission [S]; Embassy of God, Kyiv [S]) — held.
- **Evidence:**
  1. Heather J. Coleman, *Russian Baptists and Spiritual Revolution, 1905–1929* (Bloomington: Indiana UP, 2005) — V. <https://muse.jhu.edu/book/9089>
  2. Walter Sawatsky, *Soviet Evangelicals since World War II* (Herald, 1981) — repo-cited (IX.12); Russian edition «Евангельское движение в СССР после Второй мировой войны» ("The evangelical movement in the USSR after the Second World War"), Moscow 1995 — V-s.
  3. С. Н. Савинский, *История евангельских христиан-баптистов Украины, России, Белоруссии (1917–1967)* ("History of the Evangelical Christians-Baptists of Ukraine, Russia and Belarus, 1917–1967"), St Petersburg: Библия для всех, 2001 (and 1867–1917 volume, 1999) — V. <https://churchhistory.elpub.ru/jour/article/download/61/63>
  4. Catherine Wanner, *Communities of the Converted: Ukrainians and Global Evangelism* (Ithaca: Cornell UP, 2007) — V. <https://pure.psu.edu/en/publications/communities-of-the-converted-ukrainians-and-global-evangelism/>
  5. Formation of the All-Union Council of Evangelical Christians-Baptists, October 1944 — V (Ukrainian scholarly article; Britannica). <https://britannica.com/topic/Union-of-Evangelical-Christian-Baptists>
  6. 43rd Congress records, 21–23 March 1985 (UETS archive via Calisphere) — V. <https://calisphere.org/item/44907dbb548561d2ce90eafb89945ac0>; the union's confession page — V. <https://www.baptyst.com/?p=3554>
  7. William C. Fletcher, *Soviet Charismatics: The Pentecostals in the USSR* (1985) — exists, V-s; publisher [S].
  8. Library: Conybeare, *Russian Dissenters* (1921), chapter "The Stundists" — V-q (preface quoted above). Witness to 1917 only.
- **Living:** yes. Dated items seen: the Ukrainian union's confession page (undated) and a 2025 reporting piece on Ukrainian Baptists — V-s, **CR**.
- **Overlap:** VIII.51 (predecessor), IX.12 (the Initsiativniki split of 1961 stays there; E4 names it). IX.11 (Global South Pentecostals) does not reach here.
- **Recommendation:** **enter**, after the 1985 confession is read for Section A.
- **Gate question G-E4.** Where do the registered Soviet evangelicals go?
  - (a) A new family row, successor of VIII.51, with Pentecostals and the Romanian Pentecostal union as named lines. **Recommended**.
  - (b) Widen IX.12 from the dissident wing to the whole evangelical family. That merges a persecution row with a denominational one, the double-count IX.22/E1 avoids.
  - (c) Named line on VIII.51 only ("continued after 1906"), no row.

##### E3b — The Greek Church and the Ecumenical Patriarchate in the Twentieth Century (1906–c. 2010)

- **Kind:** church-family row (Church of Greece; the Ecumenical Patriarchate in Turkey).
- **Dates:** 1906 – c. 2010. Living.
- **Region / countries:** Asia Minor, Mediterranean; Greece, Turkey (Istanbul), Mount Athos.
- **Lane:** 3 Greek East & Orthodoxy.
- **Section A:** No floor question for the churches. The Old Calendarist line: Orthodox in confession; their dispute is calendar and ecclesiology — no floor question [S on each synod's text].
- **B1:** Adequate in this run: dates verified for 1907, 1924/1935, 1965; Greek-language scholarship on Zoe verified (Logothetis 2016). The Phanar's 1923–1971 record (population exchange, 1955 pogrom, Halki's closure) not verified in this run [S].
- **B2:** Strong: brotherhoods, catechism schools, Athos, the calendar schism's parallel parishes, the Phanar's minority life.
- **B3:** High. Nothing Greek after the 1860s on the tree.
- **B4:** Orthodox inquirers, ecumenists (1965), history learners.
- **B5:** Moderate.
- **Tier:** 2.
- **Named lines:** Zoe Brotherhood of Theologians (Αδελφότης Θεολόγων «Η Ζωή»), founded 1907 by Fr Eusebios Matthopoulos — four marks: beginning 1907, carriers, name, studied as a movement (Logothetis 2016; Britannica) — V/V-t; the 1960 split that formed Sotir [S]. Old Calendarists: the Church of Greece adopts the Revised Julian calendar 1924 (after the 1923 Pan-Orthodox Congress); three bishops join the Old Calendar movement in 1935 — V-t. The 1965 Joint Declaration of Paul VI and Athenagoras (7 December 1965), lifting the 1054 excommunications — V (vatican.va). The Athonite revival (1960s–) [S].
- **Evidence:**
  1. Αμαρυλλίς Λογοθέτη, *Θρησκεία και πολιτική στην μεταπολεμική Ελλάδα: η αδελφότητα «Ζωή» στις αρχές του ψυχρού πολέμου* ("Religion and politics in post-war Greece: the brotherhood 'Zoe' at the beginning of the Cold War"), PhD thesis, Panteion University, 2016 — V. <https://phdtheses.ekt.gr/eadd/handle/10442/39447>
  2. "Zoe, Greek Orthodox religious association", Britannica — V-t. <https://www.britannica.com/topic/Zoe-Greek-Orthodox-religious-association>
  3. Joint Catholic-Orthodox Declaration of Paul VI and Athenagoras I, 7 December 1965 — V. <https://www.vatican.va/content/paul-vi/en/speeches/1965/documents/hf_p-vi_spe_19651207_common-declaration.html>
  4. Old Calendarists, 1924 and 1935 — V-t (OrthodoxWiki). <https://orthodoxwiki.org/Old_Calendarists>
  5. Leustean (ed.) 2010 has an Ecumenical Patriarchate chapter — V.
  6. Zoe's periodical *Ζωή* and its catechism schools — own-voice texts, dates [S].
- **Living:** churches yes, **CR** (no dated page opened). Zoe: uncertain.
- **Overlap:** VIII.28, VIII.12 (predecessors); IX.46 (the Phanar's WCC role); IX.9.
- **Recommendation:** **enter** if G-E3 is (b); otherwise its content folds into the single family row. Hold the Phanar's 1923–71 lines for sources.
- **Gate question:** decided by G-E3.

##### E7 — Palestinian Christians — from the Nakba to Kairos (1948–c. 2010)

- **Kind:** church-region row (the Arab Christians of Palestine/Israel across several churches), with one defined movement inside it (Palestinian liberation theology / Sabeel).
- **Dates:** 1948 – c. 2010. Living.
- **Region / countries:** Middle East; Israel, the West Bank, Gaza, Jordan [S].
- **Lane:** a span row (Orthodox / Catholic / Protestant) — lane is a gate question; nearest existing: 3<->6 span.
- **Section A:** On a dated statement: *Kairos Palestine: A Moment of Truth* (Bethlehem, launched 11 December 2009), with a supporting statement of the Patriarchs and Heads of Churches in Jerusalem dated 15 December 2009. The document states its faith in Part 2, "A word of faith": "We believe in one God, a good and just God" (V-q, same PDF), and raises no floor question. The member churches are credal.
- **The movement inside it — four marks:** beginning: Ateek's *Justice and Only Justice* (Orbis, 1989), which the movement itself names as the moment the name "Palestinian Liberation Theology" was adopted, and the 1990 Tantur conference; carriers: Naim Ateek and Sabeel; name: "Palestinian Liberation Theology", "Sabeel"; recognition: O'Mahony (ed.) 1999 [chapter on the movement not confirmed]. Founding date of Sabeel: 1991 per its own page; others give 1989, 1990 or 1994 — **Contested** at the date level. Three of four marks verified; the fourth (a scholarly study naming it a movement) is V-s at best. So: named line, not a row of its own.
- **B1:** Adequate. Own-voice texts verified (Kairos); scholarship O'Mahony 1999 verified. Critics' texts (Jewish communal bodies, NGO Monitor) exist and must be carried on the same footing — the argument over the document is part of its history.
- **B2:** Adequate-to-strong: parish, pilgrimage economy, schools, emigration, occupation, ecumenical heads of churches.
- **B3:** High, and the roster's only Holy Land row is IX.13 (Messianic Judaism), so the region's Arab churches are invisible.
- **B4:** All audiences; this is one of the questions participants will most often bring.
- **B5:** Small and shrinking population [S on numbers].
- **Tier:** 2.
- **Formation confidence:** the theology of the document is **Documented** (its own text). Claims about its reception and about Sabeel's character are **Contested** and need a `contested_claim` record with both sides' published texts.
- **Evidence:**
  1. *Kairos Palestine: A Moment of Truth. A word of faith, hope and love from the heart of Palestinian suffering* (Bethlehem, 2009, "first print") — **V-q**. Para. 2.5: "We also declare that the Israeli occupation of Palestinian land is a sin against God and humanity because it deprives the Palestinians of their basic human rights, bestowed by God." The Arabic original («وقفة حق», "A moment of truth") — Arabic text **not seen**; title V-s. <https://cusj.org/wp-content/uploads/Kairos-Palestine-A-Moment-of-Truth.pdf>
  2. Naim Stifan Ateek, *Justice and Only Justice: A Palestinian Theology of Liberation* (Maryknoll: Orbis, 1989) — V (Sabeel's own newsletter). <https://sabeel.org/about/>
  3. Anthony O'Mahony (ed.), *Palestinian Christians: Religion, Politics and Society in the Holy Land* (London: Melisende, 1999) — V. <https://relbib.de/Record/321115414>
  4. Critical literature: NGO Monitor, "Sabeel's ecumenical façade" — V-s (an advocacy source; to be carried as such). <https://ngo-monitor.org/reports/_sabeel_s_ecumenical_facade_/>
  5. Mitri Raheb — named; no title verified [S].
- **Living:** yes. Sabeel's site holds a PDF uploaded April 2026 (URL dated) — V-s, **CR**.
- **Overlap:** IX.13 (Israel); III.2/VII.23 (Melkite antecedents); IX.24 (Lausanne) none.
- **Recommendation:** **enter**, with Sabeel and Kairos as named lines and a `contested_claim` record built before entry.
- **Gate question G-E7.** How should the Holy Land's Arab churches enter?
  - (a) A church-region row 1948–c. 2010 with Sabeel/Kairos as named lines and the dispute carried from both sides' texts. **Recommended**.
  - (b) A movement row for Palestinian liberation theology only. It fails mark 4 today and would tell the region through one theology.
  - (c) Hold for sources (Raheb; a study of the movement).

##### E5 — The Antiochian Orthodox Renewal — the Orthodox Youth Movement (1942–c. 2010)

- **Kind:** renewal movement within one church (like IX.10, the Coptic Sunday-School revival).
- **Dates:** 16 March 1942 – c. 2010. Living.
- **Region / countries:** Middle East; Lebanon, Syria.
- **Lane:** 3 Greek East & Orthodoxy.
- **Section A:** No floor question (Orthodox). Its own first principle — "The Orthodox Youth Movement is a spiritual Movement that calls all the Orthodox believers to a religious…" — seen only truncated in English (MECC) — not usable yet.
- **Four marks:** beginning 16 March 1942, Beirut, sixteen students of law and medicine — **V** (MECC); carriers Georges Khodr (first secretary general), Albert Laham, Ignatius (Habib) Hazim, later Patriarch Ignatius IV (patriarch from 1979) — V; name حركة الشبيبة الأرثوذكسية (Ḥarakat al-Shabība al-Urthūdhuksiyya, "Orthodox Youth Movement"), MJO — V; patriarchal charter granted by the Holy Synod, 22 January 1946 — V (MECC). Recognition by a historian: Ebeid (2020) calls it "a Movement that renewed the life of the Church in the Orthodox Patriarchate of Antioch in both Syria and Lebanon" — **V-q** — but the chapter is about Hazim; no dedicated study of the movement was verified. Mark 4 is partial.
- **B1:** Thin-to-adequate in this run. The own-voice anthology «أنطاكية تتجدد: شهادات ونصوص 1942–1992» ("Antioch renews itself: testimonies and texts, 1942–1992") is named in Arabic search results — [S].
- **B2:** Adequate: parish circles, catechesis, Balamand (theology institute; university from the 1980s [V-s]), Syndesmos (1953, V-t).
- **B3:** High. No Arab Orthodox row after era 8.
- **B4:** Orthodox inquirers, ecumenists, those asking about Christians in the Arab world.
- **B5:** Small.
- **Tier:** 2 (on entry).
- **Evidence:**
  1. MECC, "The Day the Orthodox Youth Movement Entered the History of the Church of Antioch" (posted 17 March [2025]) — V. <https://www.mecc.org/mecc/2025/3/20/march-16-1942>
  2. Metropolitan Georges Khodr, official biography — V. <https://georgeskhodr.org/?p=182>
  3. Bishara Ebeid, "Greek Orthodox Patriarch Ignatius Hazim (1979–2012) of Antioch and his Perspective on Inter-Christian and Interreligious Dialogue", in a Harrassowitz volume (ISBN 978-3-447-11456-1), pp. 53ff. — **V-q** (Ca' Foscari repository PDF read). Volume title, editors and year [S].
  4. Ignatius IV Hazim, *The Resurrection and Modern Man* (Crestwood: SVS Press, 1985) — V-s (cited by Ebeid).
- **Living:** yes. MECC, 8 December 2025: Patriarch John X meets the movement's general conference — V-s (URL dated); **CD** candidate once opened.
- **Overlap:** IX.9 (Hazim studied at St Sergius, 1945 — V-q via Ebeid); IX.10 (parallel Coptic revival).
- **Recommendation:** **hold for sources** — one dedicated study and the movement's founding texts in Arabic. Then enter.
- **Gate question G-E5.**
  - (a) Enter now as a Tier 2 renewal-movement row on the verified marks 1–3 and the Ebeid line.
  - (b) Hold until a scholarly study of the movement and its 1942 principles in Arabic are read. **Recommended**: the evidence rule asks for both; mark 4 is partial.
  - (c) Make it a named line inside a wider "Arab Christianity of Lebanon and Syria" row with E6.

##### E8 — The Romanian Greek Catholic Church — Suppression and Return (1948–c. 2010)

- **Kind:** church body (Eastern Catholic).
- **Dates:** 1948 (dissolution) – c. 2010; the church itself dates from 1700 [S] and has no earlier row.
- **Region / countries:** Romania (Transylvania).

- **Section A:** No floor question.
- **B1:** Adequate. Decree 358 of 1 December 1948 (published 2 December) dissolved the church; property passed to the state and its churches to the Orthodox church — V. Decree-Law 9 of 31 December 1989 restored it; Holy See recognizes its metropolitan 12 March 1990 — V (jurnalul.ro). The imprisoned bishops (Iuliu Hossu and others) — [S] in this run.
- **B2:** Adequate: clandestine clergy, lay underground, prison testimony, property disputes after 1990.
- **B3:** Moderate-high: IX.21 tells the same pattern for Ukraine.
- **B4:** Catholics, Orthodox, history learners.
- **Tier:** 2.
- **Evidence:** Decree 358/1948 and Decree-Law 9/1989 — V. <https://jurnalul.ro/special-jurnalul/discordie-intre-ortodocsi-si-greco-catolici-542201.html>; Romanian: «Desființarea Bisericii Române Unite (1948) în memoria elitei bisericești» ("The dissolution of the Romanian United Church (1948) in the memory of the church elite"), CEEOL — V-s. For the neighboring liquidations: Jaroslav Coranič, "The Liquidation of the Greek Catholic Church in Communist Czechoslovakia, 1948–50", *Journal of Ecclesiastical History* 72.3 (2021), 590–610 — V (Prešov "sobor", 28 April 1950, "Action P"). <https://www.cambridge.org/core/journals/journal-of-ecclesiastical-history/article/liquidation-of-the-greek-catholic-church-in-communist-czechoslovakia-194850/7CC6ADC552F187D7B042EB346BE15194>. Mukachevo 1949; Bishop Theodore Romzha killed 1947 — V-s (one source).
- **Living:** yes, **CR** (2025 local reporting on Blaj cathedral).
- **Recommendation:** **enter** as Tier 2; Czechoslovak (Prešov 1950) and Transcarpathian (Mukachevo 1949) as named lines in IX.21.
- **Gate question G-E8.** (a) New row for the Romanian church, the other two as IX.21 lines. **Recommended.** (b) Rename and widen IX.21 to "The Suppressed Greek Catholic Churches, 1946–1990" (a change to an existing row). (c) Named lines in IX.21 only.

- **Kind:** church bodies (UAOC 1921; its 1942 revival; diaspora churches; UAOC re-founded 1989–90; Kyiv Patriarchate 1992 [S]).

- **Recommendation:** **named line in E1 now; own row held** for the 1921 and 1990 own-voice texts.

## 6b. Supplementary pass (after Round 1): places the regional passes missed

The supplementary pass's `candidates.md`, as written, with its headings demoted two levels. Its gate questions G-SUP-1 to G-SUP-15 are numbered G-136 to G-150 in section 7, and its wanted items are in section 8.

### Era 10 global completeness sweep — supplementary pass (places the four regional passes missed), 1906 to c. 2010

Researcher: Opus 5.5, gs-supp. Branch `claude/era10-global-sweep`, read only. Scratch research for the gate, not census copy. No metered model calls. No repo file edited.

**Why this pass.** The Round 1 review of `CiC_Step0_Era10_GlobalSweep_V1_0.md` (M16, "Balance") named places the regional passes never covered: Southeast Asia beyond Vietnam, Indonesia and the Philippines; South Asia beyond India; Central Asia and Mongolia; Arab and Iranian evangelicals; Latin American indigenous Christianity and Cuba after 1959. This pass covers them, every river, under the same standard, evidence rule and non-English source rule (Decision-Log, "Era 10 global completeness sweep, by region and by river", 2026-11-09).

**Read first.** The sweep record (all 59 candidates, sections 2 to 8), the Round 1 review, and `cic-website/data/world-census.json` era 10 (56 rows) and era 9. No era 10 row carries Thailand, Malaysia, Singapore, Cambodia, Laos, Nepal, Bangladesh, Sri Lanka, Pakistan (beyond IX.32's bus journey), Mongolia, Central Asia (beyond IX.22's 1937 Chimkent shootings), Persian-speaking Christians, Arab Protestants, Maya or Andean Christians, or Cuba (beyond IX.21's missile-crisis context). The sweep's candidates touch some of these: GS-A2 (Church of Pakistan line; Sri Lanka and Bangladesh [S]), GS-A3 (Punjab), GS-A12 (CPM), GS-A19 (Myanmar, held), LA-1 (Cuba in its country list), LA-12 (Guatemala line). Each candidate below says which row or candidate it touches.

**The Library.** `cic/texts/REGISTRY.yaml` holds no in-era file for any of these places (searched for Thai, Malay, Burmese, Nepali, Sinhala, Pakistani, Persian, Egyptian, Mongolian, Cuban, Maya and Andean holdings). Every source below is outside the Library.

**Non-English sources.** Searched in Spanish, Thai, Persian, Arabic and French, with original-language titles cited and glossed. Found and cited: Falla 1978 and Gómez Treto 1987 (Spanish, by Guatemalan and Cuban scholars), Andrade 2004 and Guamán (Spanish, Ecuador), Prasit 1984 (Thai), Dehqani-Tafti 1992 (Persian), Badr 2005 (Arabic original), Peyrouse 2003 and Ponchaud 1990 (French), the Punjabi Zabur 1908 ([S]). Not reached: Burmese, Khmer, Lao, Malay, Nepali, Bengali, Sinhala, Tamil, Urdu, Mongolian, Kazakh and Maya-language texts; each is on the wanted list.

**Verification key.** **V** = bibliographic detail or dated fact confirmed on a page opened in this run (raw text or page metadata), URL given. **V-q** = quotation read verbatim in downloaded raw page text. **V-s** = seen only in a search-result listing or summary; never counts as verified. **[S]** = not confirmed in this run. **wanted** = searched for, not found, not cited. Renderings marked [my rendering] are this researcher's own. Living marks follow R3: CD only for a verified independent source dated after 2010; CR for the body's own word, journalism, or an unverified source; "none" where no dated item after 2010 was read.

**The honest result.** Four places carry a row candidate on verified sources (Maya Christianity, Iran, Cuba, Pakistan). Most others come out as a hold or a named line in an existing candidate. That is said where it is true.

##### 1. SUP-1 — Maya Christianity in the twentieth century: Catholic Action catechists, the Diocese of San Cristóbal, the Guatemalan war and the Maya Protestant churches

- **Kind:** regional window (church-region row, Catholic spine with a Protestant wing); assessed as a church row
- **Dates:** c. 1948-c. 2010. Falla's study opens in 1948, when Acción Católica reached San Antonio Ilotenango, Quiché (title dates 1948-1970, V). Anchors inside the window: the Indigenous Congress of San Cristóbal de las Casas, October 1974 (exact days 12-15 or 13-15 differ, [S]); the expulsions of Protestant Chamulas from 1974 ([S] on the start year); the archdiocese's REMHI report Guatemala: Nunca Más presented 24 April 1998, and Bishop Juan Gerardi killed 26 April 1998 (V-s).
- **Region / countries:** Latin America — Guatemala, Mexico (Chiapas)
- **Lane:** 5 Latin West & Catholicism (Protestant wing as a named line, lane 6/7)
- **Section A:** Catholic spine: no question (Catholic). The Protestant wing (Presbyterian, Pentecostal and independent Maya congregations) is per body, and no dated statement of faith was seen for any of them. If the row is entered with the Catholic spine and the Protestant wing as a named line, Section A is clear for the row.
- **B1:** Good. Ricardo Falla, a Guatemalan Jesuit anthropologist, Quiché rebelde (Editorial Universitaria, 1978, V-s; English edition, UT Press, V); Christine Kovic, Mayan Voices for Human Rights (UT Press, 2005; V), built on Maya testimony; Garrard-Burnett 1998 (V, already in LA-5). The REMHI report is the church's own dated text. Asymmetry: Maya-language own texts were not found in this run.
- **B2:** Full: the cofradía and costumbre against the catechists, conversion as rupture (Falla's 'conversión rebelde'), land and cooperatives, the 1974 Congress, war and massacre, displacement and expulsion, Maya Protestant congregations, Bible translation.
- **B3:** High. No era 10 row has an indigenous American subject. LA-1 names no Maya, Chiapas or Guatemala content; IX.8 tells Rutilio Grande's catechists in El Salvador, not the Maya; LA-12 is a one-line pointer to Guatemalan evangelical growth.
- **B4:** Latin American Catholics and Protestants, indigenous and diaspora readers, historians, pastors (catechesis and power), seekers asking about the church and violence.
- **B5:** Large (millions of Maya Christians); tiebreak only.
- **Tier:** Tier 2
- **Evidence:**
  - Ricardo Falla, Quiché rebelde: estudio de un movimiento de conversión religiosa, rebelde a las creencias tradicionales, en San Antonio Ilotenango, Quiché (1948-1970) ('Rebel Quiché: a study of a movement of religious conversion, rebellious against the traditional beliefs, in San Antonio Ilotenango, Quiché, 1948-1970'), Guatemala: Editorial Universitaria, 1978 — *regional scholarship (Spanish, by a Guatemalan scholar)* — **V-s (Spanish imprint) / V (English edition)** — <https://utpress.utexas.edu/9780292725324/quiche-rebelde/> — The UT Press page (opened) gives the English edition, tr. Phillip Berryman, LLILAS series, and says the book 'examines what happened when Acción Católica came into the Guatemalan municipio of San Antonio Ilotenango, Quiché'. The 1978 Spanish imprint and full title come from a bibliography seen in a search result; the English edition's year (2001 or 2003) differs across sources.
  - Christine Kovic, Mayan Voices for Human Rights: Displaced Catholics in Highland Chiapas (Austin: University of Texas Press, 2005) — *scholarly study* — **V** — <https://utpress.utexas.edu/9780292706408/mayan-voices-for-human-rights/> — Page opened: print May 2005, 248 pp.
  - Oficina de Derechos Humanos del Arzobispado de Guatemala (ODHAG), Guatemala: Nunca Más. Informe del Proyecto Interdiocesano de Recuperación de la Memoria Histórica (REMHI) ('Guatemala: Never Again. Report of the Inter-diocesan Project for the Recovery of Historical Memory'), presented 24 April 1998 — *own dated text (the archdiocese's)* — **V-s** — <https://www.prensalibre.com/?p=383948> — Date of presentation and Gerardi's death (26 April 1998) consistent across news results; the report itself was not opened. Wanted: the report's own text and its chapters on catechists.
  - Congreso Indígena, San Cristóbal de las Casas, October 1974 (Tzotzil, Tzeltal, Tojolabal and Chol delegates; prepared with Bishop Samuel Ruiz and the diocese's catechists) — *dated own act* — **[S]** — <https://schoolsforchiapas.org/50-years-of-the-indigenous-congress-plowing-for-peace-in-the-midst-of-war/> — Days differ (12-15 or 13-15 October). The Congress's own acuerdos were not found. Wanted.
  - Carolina Rivera Farfán, article on evangelical organizations in the Altos de Chiapas (Chamula as 'the expelling municipality par excellence') — *regional scholarship (Spanish)* — **V-s** — <https://dialnet.unirioja.es/descarga/articulo/6092361.pdf> — Title, journal and year not confirmed in this run. Manuela Cantón Delgado's reading that the expulsions were 'only in part religious' was seen only in a search summary [S].
  - Virginia Garrard-Burnett, Protestantism in Guatemala: Living in the New Jerusalem (Austin: University of Texas Press, 1998) — *scholarly study* — **V (as verified in LA-5, item 6)** — Carries the Protestant wing; already on the record.
- **Living today:** True; newest dated evidence: 2024 (V-s); Schools for Chiapas, '50 Years of the Indigenous Congress' (NGO page, 2024 anniversary; V-s). Grade: CR.
- **Overlap:** LA-1 (continental Catholic row; names no Maya, Guatemala or Chiapas content). IX.8 (base communities; Rutilio Grande's catechists, El Salvador). LA-12 (pointer line on Guatemalan evangelical growth, Garrard-Burnett). LA-5 (Protestant family). IX.11 (Pentecostal family). None holds an indigenous American subject.
- **Contested:** 'Genocide' as the name for the Guatemalan army's campaign against Maya communities is a legal and historical dispute: the 2013 national verdict was annulled (post-2010, so only as later scholarship). A contested_claim record is needed before any copy uses the word. The causes of the Chamula expulsions (religious or political-economic) are Contested.
- **Recommendation:** enter once (two conditions: a dated own text of the catechists or of the 1974 Congress staged, for example the Congress's acuerdos or a REMHI chapter on catechists; and the contested_claim record on 'genocide' built). Tier 2. LA-12 then moves in as this row's Protestant line or stays in LA-5 (the gate's call). (class: enter once)
- **Gate question:** How should the Maya churches of Guatemala and Chiapas enter era 10?
  - (a) One regional window row, c. 1948-c. 2010, Catholic spine (Catholic Action, the catechists, San Cristóbal under Samuel Ruiz, REMHI and Gerardi), with the Maya Protestant churches and the Chamula expulsions as a named line; lane 5.
  - (b) Named lines only: the catechists and REMHI in LA-1, the Protestant side in LA-12/LA-5.
  - (c) Hold until a Maya-language or Maya-authored own text is found.
  - **Recommended:** (a), once a dated own text is staged and the contested_claim record is built. (b) would leave era 10 with no indigenous American subject, the silence the review named; (c) sets a bar no other row meets, since Kovic's study is built on Maya testimony and Falla's on fieldwork in K'iche' communities.

##### 2. SUP-2 — The Persian-speaking church of Iran: the Anglican diocese, the Evangelical and Assemblies of God churches, and Muslim-background believers (1912-c. 2010)

- **Kind:** church family (national), assessed as a church row
- **Dates:** 1912-c. 2010. 1912: the Anglican diocese of Persia constituted with Charles Stileman's consecration [S, date July 1912 from encyclopedic sources]. Inside the window: Hassan Dehqani-Tafti, the first ethnic Persian bishop, from 1961 [S on the day]; the 1979 revolution, and his son Bahram killed in 1980 (the bishop's book, V-s); Mehdi Dibaj released in January 1994 and the Assemblies of God's Haik Hovsepian Mehr killed that month (Amnesty, 1994, V-s; dates differ: 19 or 30 January).
- **Region / countries:** Middle East — Iran, diaspora (UK, USA, Turkey, Armenia)
- **Lane:** 6 Protestant & Evangelical (the Assemblies of God and Jama'at-e Rabbani as a lane 7 line)
- **Section A:** Not yet assessable as one row. The Anglican diocese clears through the Anglican formularies (A5), as GS-A11's Brotherhood does. The Iranian Assemblies of God's Persian-language statement of faith was not found; the US Statement of Fundamental Truths is not its own text. The Evangelical (Presbyterian) Church of Iran: not seen.
- **B1:** Moderate. Robin E. Waterfield, Christians in Persia (1973; V, a Church History review opened); Mark Bradley, Iran and Christianity (Continuum, 2008; V-s; the author is linked to mission work [S]); Benedikt Römer, The Iranian Christian Diaspora (I.B. Tauris, 2024; V), later scholarship that uses Persian-language church magazines. Encyclopaedia Iranica entries on Protestant missions and the Episcopal Church in Persia (pointers). Own voice: Dehqani-Tafti's English memoir The Hard Awakening (1981) and his Persian history Masīḥ wa masīḥīyat-i nazd-i īrāniyān (1992).
- **B2:** Good: Persian worship in a land where the law protects only Armenian and Assyrian churches, mission hospitals and schools (Isfahan, Shiraz, Kerman), conversion and apostasy charges, the revolution's confiscations, murdered pastors, house churches, satellite broadcasting [S], diaspora congregations.
- **B3:** High. The roster has Iran only through IX.19's Urmia Assyrians (Syriac river) and IX.27's Armenians. No row carries Persian-speaking Christians, and the Muslim-background church of Iran is one of the most-cited late-century growth stories in the Muslim world.
- **B4:** Iranian and diaspora Christians, converts from Islam, historians, pastors, thoughtful believers (persecution and witness).
- **B5:** Small to mid; contested numbers; tiebreak only.
- **Tier:** Tier 2
- **Evidence:**
  - Robin E. Waterfield, Christians in Persia: Assyrians, Armenians, Roman Catholics and Protestants (London: Allen & Unwin, 1973; New York: Barnes and Noble, 1973), 192 pp. — *scholarly survey* — **V** — <https://www.cambridge.org/core/journals/church-history/article/christians-in-persia-assyrians-armenians-roman-catholics-and-protestants-by-robin-e-waterfield-new-york-barnes-and-noble-1973-192-pp-1175/82EAAA3441D019903A04CBBDED0D17B5> — Review by R. Pierce Beaver, Church History 43 (1974): 129, opened. Allen & Unwin imprint from catalog results (V-s).
  - Mark Bradley, Iran and Christianity: Historical Identity and Present Relevance (London and New York: Continuum, 2008) — *study* — **V-s** — <https://library.usi.edu/record/432234> — Catalog seen in search results only. Its claim that more Iranian Muslims became Christians in 25 years than since the seventh century is Contested and must not be stated as settled.
  - Benedikt Römer, The Iranian Christian Diaspora: Religion and Nationhood in Exile (London and New York: I.B. Tauris, 2024) — *scholarly study (later scholarship on in-era matters)* — **V** — <https://www.torrossa.com/fr/resources/an/5869083> — Record opened: I.B. Tauris, 2024.
  - H. B. Dehqani-Tafti, The Hard Awakening (London: SPCK/Triangle; New York: Seabury, 1981) — *own voice (memoir of the first Persian Anglican bishop)* — **V-s** — <https://vufind.daystar.ac.ke/Record/219814/Details> — Publisher varies by catalog (SPCK, Triangle, Seabury). Title from a line of Hafez [S].
  - Hassan B. Dehqani-Tafti, مسیح و مسیحیت نزد ایرانیان, Masīḥ wa masīḥīyat-i nazd-i īrāniyān ('Christ and Christianity amongst the Iranians'), London: Sohrab Books, 1992- — *own-language history (Persian)* — **V-s** — <https://relbib.de/Record/1129203972> — The catalog page blocked automated access; record seen in search result. Not read.
  - Amnesty International, Iran report on Hovsepian Mehr and Dibaj (AI Index MDE 13/07/94), 1994 — *contemporary independent record* — **V-s** — <https://www.amnesty.org/es/wp-content/uploads/2021/06/mde130071994en.pdf> — Located; not opened.
  - Encyclopaedia Iranica, 'Christianity viii. Christian missions in Persia' and 'Episcopal Church in Persia' — *reference (pointer)* — **V-s** — <https://www.iranicaonline.org/?p=8921> — Encyclopedic; a pointer, not an anchor.
  - A Persian-language statement of faith of the Iranian Assemblies of God or of the Evangelical Church of Iran; the diocese's Persian prayer book — *own dated statement* — **wanted** — Searched; not found.
- **Living today:** True; newest dated evidence: 2024 (Römer, I.B. Tauris; V); Römer 2024 (V; independent scholarly study of the diaspora churches today). Article 18 and Anglican News reports (2013 onward) are journalism or the body's own word. Grade: CD.
- **Overlap:** IX.19 (Urmia, Syriac river; the Assyrian Evangelical and Pentecostal congregations sit at its edge [S]). IX.27 (Armenians). IX.52 (Anglican Communion; no Iran content). E-region held item 'Arab and Iranian evangelical churches, not researched'. None holds the Persian-speaking church.
- **Contested:** Convert numbers (Bradley's claim) are Contested. Responsibility for the 1994 killings: the state blamed the Mojahedin-e Khalq [S]; Iranian Protestants and Amnesty pointed to extrajudicial execution. Both readings must be carried, not one asserted.
- **Recommendation:** enter once (Section A settled: the diocese through the Anglican formularies, plus one dated Persian-language statement of the Assemblies of God or the Evangelical Church), Tier 2, with a contested_claim record on numbers and on the 1994 killings. (class: enter once)
- **Gate question:** How should Iran's Persian-speaking Protestants enter era 10?
  - (a) One national church-family row, 1912-c. 2010: the Anglican diocese and Dehqani-Tafti, the Evangelical Church, the Assemblies of God and the 1994 killings, the house churches and the diaspora; lane 6.
  - (b) A named line in IX.19, which already tags Iran.
  - (c) Hold for Persian-language sources.
  - **Recommended:** (a), once Section A is settled. (b) would file Persian-speaking converts from Islam inside a Syriac genocide-and-diaspora row they do not belong to; (c) is the fallback if no Persian-language statement is found.

##### 3. SUP-3 — The churches in revolutionary Cuba (1959-c. 2010)

- **Kind:** church-region row (Catholic and Protestant), assessed as a church row
- **Dates:** 1959-c. 2010. 1959: the revolution. Inside the window: the expulsion of priests in September 1961 [S]; the Encuentro Nacional Eclesial Cubano (ENEC), Havana, 17-23 February 1986, after the five-year Reflexión Eclesial Cubana (V-s; John Paul II's message of 11 February 1986 on vatican.va, V-s); 21-25 January 1998 (the 21 January arrival speech V-q; the visit's end date V-s); the 1990s Pentecostal growth (Corse 2007, V).
- **Region / countries:** Latin America — Cuba
- **Lane:** 5<->6 bridge (Latin West / Protestant)
- **Section A:** Catholic: no question. Protestant wing per body. A 1977 confession of the Iglesia Presbiteriana-Reformada en Cuba was searched for and not found (wanted). The Brill 'Archives of the Presbyterian Church in Cuba Online' collection exists and may hold it.
- **B1:** Good. A Cuban scholar: Raúl Gómez Treto, La Iglesia católica durante la construcción del socialismo en Cuba (San José: DEI, 1987; written for CEHILA; V). John M. Kirk, Between God and the Party (University of South Florida Press, 1989; V). Theron Corse, Protestants, Revolution, and the Cuba-U.S. Bond (University Press of Florida, 2007; V). Own texts: the ENEC final document (1986, not opened), the bishops' documents collected by Palabra Nueva.
- **B2:** Full: a church under the only communist state in the Americas, the 1961 break, emigration, Catholic schools closed, Protestant seminaries (Matanzas), the 1986 ENEC, the 1992 constitutional change [S], the 1998 papal visit, house churches and Pentecostal growth, Santería beside Catholic devotion (the Virgen de la Caridad).
- **B3:** High. LA-1 lists Cuba in its countries and B2, but carries no Cuban event. The East's rows tell churches under communism in Europe; nothing tells one in the Americas.
- **B4:** Cuban and diaspora Catholics and Protestants, historians, readers asking how churches lived under communism.
- **B5:** Mid; tiebreak only.
- **Tier:** Tier 2
- **Evidence:**
  - Raúl Gómez Treto, La Iglesia católica durante la construcción del socialismo en Cuba ('The Catholic Church during the building of socialism in Cuba'), San José, Costa Rica: DEI, 1987 (2nd ed. 1989) — *regional scholarship (Spanish, by a Cuban scholar, for CEHILA)* — **V** — <https://dl.atla.com/concern/works/1n79h755x?locale=en> — Atla record opened: DEI, 1987. Full text of the 1989 edition at archive.org (laiglesiacatolic00gome).
  - John M. Kirk, Between God and the Party: Religion and Politics in Revolutionary Cuba (Tampa/Gainesville: University of South Florida Press, 1989), xxi + 231 pp. — *scholarly study* — **V** — <https://cambridge.org/core/journals/americas/article/between-god-and-the-party-religion-and-politics-in-revolutionary-cuba-by-john-m-kirk-gainesville-university-of-south-florida-press-1989-pp-xxi-231-tables-notes-bibliography-index-2200-cloth-1500-paper/896646F3998698240E045EF4BCDD354E> — Review by Daniel H. Levine, The Americas 46 (1990): 407, opened.
  - Theron Corse, Protestants, Revolution, and the Cuba-U.S. Bond (Gainesville: University Press of Florida, 2007), xi + 195 pp. — *scholarly study* — **V** — <https://www.cambridge.org/core/journals/journal-of-latin-american-studies/article/abs/theron-corse-protestants-revolution-and-the-cubaus-bond-gainesville-fl-university-press-of-florida-2007-pp-xi195-5995-hb/8C879BE045CBF26FF127D9A70AECF0B8> — Review by Daniel R. Miller, JLAS 41 (2009): 177, opened.
  - Encuentro Nacional Eclesial Cubano (ENEC), Documento final ('Final document'), Havana, February 1986; the bishops' pastoral instruction promulgating it, May 1986 — *own dated text* — **V-s** — <https://www.palabranueva.net/en/enec/> — Event and dates seen in search results; the document itself not opened. Wanted.
  - John Paul II, arrival speech, Havana airport, 21 January 1998 — *dated text (papal, not Cuban own voice)* — **V-q** — <https://www.vatican.va/content/john-paul-ii/es/speeches/1998/january/documents/hf_jp-ii_spe_19980121_lahavana-arrival.html> — Quote checked against the downloaded raw page.
  - Iglesia Presbiteriana-Reformada en Cuba, Confesión de Fe (1977) — *own dated statement* — **wanted** — <https://primarysources.brillonline.com/browse/archives-of-the-presbyterian-church-in-cuba-online> — Searched; not found. The Brill archive is a likely holder.
- **Quotes (original, then rendering):**
  - (Spanish) “Que Cuba se abra con todas sus magníficas posibilidades al mundo y que el mundo se abra a Cuba” Rendering: “May Cuba open itself to the world with all its magnificent possibilities, and may the world open itself to Cuba. [my rendering]” — John Paul II, Havana, 21 January 1998 — **V-q (raw page text)**
- **Living today:** True; newest dated evidence: 2026 (V-s); Diario de Cuba on the ENEC's 40th anniversary, 2026 (journalism, V-s); PC(USA) news on the Cuban Presbyterian archives, 24 September 2025 (V-s). Grade: CR.
- **Overlap:** LA-1 lists Cuba among its countries and in B2 but tells no Cuban event; if LA-1 enters, this row is its child as the Cristero row would be. LA-5 (Protestant family; no Cuba content). IX.21's text mentions the Cuban missile crisis only as context for Slipyj's release.
- **Recommendation:** enter once (the ENEC final document opened, and the Protestant wing's Section A settled on one dated statement), Tier 2. (class: enter once)
- **Gate question:** How should the Cuban churches after 1959 enter?
  - (a) Their own row, 1959-c. 2010, Catholic and Protestant together (lane 5<->6), as a child of LA-1 if LA-1 enters.
  - (b) A named line in LA-1 for the Catholic side and one in LA-5 for the Protestants.
  - (c) Hold until the ENEC document and a Protestant statement are read.
  - **Recommended:** (a). Once the ENEC final document is opened and the Protestant wing's Section A is settled on one dated statement. Three verified studies, one of them Cuban, carry it, and the subject is one story told from two rivers: churches under communism in the Americas. (b) splits it, and LA-1 and LA-5 are themselves not yet entered.

##### 4. SUP-4 — The Christians of Pakistan (1947-c. 2010)

- **Kind:** church-region row (Catholic and the Church of Pakistan, with Presbyterian and other bodies), assessed as a church row
- **Dates:** 1947-c. 2010. 1947: Partition. Roots: the Punjabi Chuhra mass movements (GS-A3) and Imam-ud-Din Shahbaz's Punjabi metrical psalter, the Punjabi Zabur, published 1908 [S]. Inside the window: the Church of Pakistan, 1 November 1970 [S]; the blasphemy provision of 1986 [S]; Bishop John Joseph's death in protest at Sahiwal, May 1998 (Walbridge, V).
- **Region / countries:** Greater Asia — Pakistan
- **Lane:** 5<->6 bridge (Latin West / Protestant)
- **Section A:** Catholic: no question. The Church of Pakistan: through its Basis of Union, not seen (the same state as GS-A2's CNI). The Presbyterian bodies: Westminster standards [S].
- **B1:** Moderate. Linda S. Walbridge, The Christians of Pakistan: The Passion of Bishop John Joseph (RoutledgeCurzon, 2003; V). John O'Brien, The Construction of Pakistani Christian Identity (Lahore: Research Society of Pakistan, [2006?]; V). Yousaf Sadiq on the Punjabi Zabur (2020 book; Middlesex PhD 2015) [S]. Own-language texts: the Zabur (Punjabi, 1908) [S on the edition]. Urdu church records: wanted.
- **B2:** Good: a mostly Dalit-origin Punjabi church, Punjabi psalm singing in folk ragas, mission schools and colleges, a minority inside an Islamic republic, blasphemy cases, the 1997 Shantinagar attack [S], the 2009 Gojra attack [S].
- **B3:** High. GS-A2 makes the Church of Pakistan a named line in a South Indian union row and GS-A3 tells the mass movements to c. 1940. Neither tells the church after Partition.
- **B4:** Pakistani and diaspora Christians, historians, pastors, readers asking about minority faith under blasphemy law.
- **B5:** Mid (about 2 percent of Pakistan [S]); tiebreak only.
- **Tier:** Tier 2
- **Evidence:**
  - Linda S. Walbridge, The Christians of Pakistan: The Passion of Bishop John Joseph (London: RoutledgeCurzon, 2003) — *scholarly study* — **V** — <https://www.routledge.com/The-Christians-of-Pakistan-The-Passion-of-Bishop-John-Joseph/Walbridge/p/book/9780415570886> — Publisher page opened: copyright 2003; describes John Joseph's death in May 1998.
  - John O'Brien, The Construction of Pakistani Christian Identity (Lahore: Research Society of Pakistan, [2006?]), Subaltern Studies Series 1 — *regional scholarship (published in Lahore)* — **V** — <https://ci.nii.ac.jp/ncid/BA85637418?l=en> — CiNii record opened; year bracketed in the record.
  - Imam-ud-Din Shahbaz, ਪੰਜਾਬੀ ਜ਼ਬੂਰ / پنجابی زبور, Punjabi Zabur: Desi Raagan Vich ('The Punjabi Psalms in native ragas'), 1908 — *own-language founding text of Punjabi Christian worship* — **[S]** — <https://tribune.com.pk/story/2616584/a-raag-beyond-borders-and-belief> — Date and title from journalism and encyclopedic pages; an edition not seen. Script forms of the title not confirmed.
  - Yousaf Sadiq, The Contextualized Psalms (Punjabi Zabur): A Precious Heritage of the Global Punjabi Christian Community (2020) — *scholarly study (later scholarship)* — **V-s** — <https://discover.library.unt.edu/catalog/b6879327> — Catalog in search result; publisher not confirmed.
  - Church of Pakistan, Basis of Union (1970) — *own dated statement* — **wanted** — Not found.
- **Living today:** True; newest dated evidence: 2020 (V-s); Sadiq 2020 reports the Zabur still sung across Punjabi congregations (V-s); Express Tribune feature on the Zabur (journalism, date not fixed). Grade: CR.
- **Overlap:** GS-A2 (Church of Pakistan as a named line under G-103(a)). GS-A3 (Punjab mass movements to c. 1940). IX.32 names Pakistan only on Newbigin's bus journey. If this row enters, G-103(a)'s Church of Pakistan line moves here with a cross-reference.
- **Contested:** Walbridge's publisher states that John Joseph, 'the first native Pakistani Catholic bishop, shot himself in front of the courthouse where a Christian had been sentenced to death for blasphemy' (V-q, routledge.com); church accounts frame the death as a protest witness [S]. Both framings must be carried, and the death of a real person handled with care.
- **Recommendation:** enter once (Section A: the Church of Pakistan's Basis of Union opened), Tier 2. (class: enter once)
- **Gate question:** Where do Pakistan's Christians after 1947 go?
  - (a) Their own church-region row, 1947-c. 2010, with the Zabur and the mass movements as its roots; G-103(a)'s Church of Pakistan line moves here.
  - (b) Keep the Church of Pakistan as a named line in GS-A2 and add the Catholic and blasphemy-law story there.
  - (c) Hold for Urdu and Punjabi own-voice sources.
  - **Recommended:** (a). Once the Church of Pakistan's Basis of Union is opened for Section A. GS-A2 is a South Indian union story; Pakistan's church lived a different life as a minority in an Islamic state, and Walbridge carries it. (b) would overload a row about church union with a story about persecution.

##### 5. SUP-5 — The Nepali church (1951-c. 2010)

- **Kind:** church family (one national family of independent and mission-planted congregations), assessed as a church row
- **Dates:** 1951-c. 2010. 1951: Nepal opens to foreigners. The Ramghat church in Pokhara, 1952 [S], under the Nepal Evangelistic Band. Changing religion was illegal 'until November 1990' (Yale exhibit, V-q); growth after the 1990 constitution [S].
- **Region / countries:** Greater Asia — Nepal, India (Darjeeling, the Nepali-speaking roots)
- **Lane:** 6 Protestant & Evangelical (with a lane 7 Pentecostal-charismatic line)
- **Section A:** Not yet assessable. No dated statement of faith of a Nepali church body or of the National Churches Fellowship of Nepal was found.
- **B1:** Thin to moderate. Cindy L. Perry, A Biographical History of the Church in Nepal (Kathmandu: Nepal Church History Project, 1989; V), the church's own history project; Perry's Edinburgh PhD on the Protestant expansion among the Nepali diaspora (V-s; year 1994 [S]). The Nepal Church History Project archive at Yale Divinity Library (RG 215; V) holds the primary record. No independent university-press study verified.
- **B2:** Good: Nepali believers, not missionaries, planting the church (Perry's argument, V-s), imprisonment for conversion before 1990, healing and prayer, caste and ethnic minorities, the Darjeeling hinterland, mission hospitals (United Mission to Nepal) [S].
- **B3:** High. The church grew from almost nothing after 1951 and fast after 1990; nothing on the roster carries Nepal.
- **B4:** Nepali and diaspora Christians, mission historians, readers asking how a church grows under a ban.
- **B5:** Mid, numbers Contested (census 1.4 percent in 2011 [S] against church estimates of one million or more [S]).
- **Tier:** Tier 3 now; Tier 2 with one independent study
- **Evidence:**
  - Cindy L. Perry, A Biographical History of the Church in Nepal (Kathmandu: Nepal Church History Project, 1989) — *history (the church's own project)* — **V** — <https://vufind.daystar.ac.ke/Record/217811> — Catalog opened: Kathmandu, NCHP, 1989. Other sources give 1990 or 2000 for later editions.
  - 'Christianity in Nepal: Documentation from the Day Missions Collection', Yale Divinity School Library exhibit; Nepal Church History Project records, RG 215 — *archive (primary record)* — **V** — <https://divinity-adhoc.library.yale.edu/Exhibits/Nepal_exhibit/Nepal2.htm> — Page opened.
  - Cindy Perry, 'The history of the expansion of Protestant Christianity among the Nepali diaspora', PhD thesis, University of Edinburgh — *scholarly study (thesis)* — **V-s** — <https://era.ed.ac.uk/items/fa20aa29-b6be-4ffe-a470-67db2a4b3e33/full> — Repository page refused access (403); title from search result.
  - Nepali-language own texts (church records, hymnals, testimonies) in the NCHP archive — *own-language sources* — **wanted** — Not searched in Nepali in this run beyond the archive listing.
- **Quotes (original, then rendering):**
  - (English) “Until November 1990, changing religion was illegal. The law authorized severe penalties for attempting to convert another person.” — Yale Divinity School Library, 'Christianity in Nepal' exhibit — **V-q (raw page text; an exhibit, not an own text)**
- **Living today:** True; newest dated evidence: 2011 ([S]); 2011 census figure of 1.4 percent Christian (via encyclopedic page, [S]). Grade: CR.
- **Overlap:** None. IX.11 (Global South Pentecostal) names no Nepali body. GS-A2's CNI touches Darjeeling only.
- **Contested:** Numbers of Christians are Contested.
- **Recommendation:** hold for sources: one independent scholarly study and one dated statement of faith; send the NCHP archive to the source-research thread. (class: hold)
- **Gate question:** What happens to the Nepali church?
  - (a) Hold, and send the Nepal Church History Project archive (Yale RG 215) and Perry's thesis to source research.
  - (b) Enter now as a Tier 3 row on Perry.
  - (c) A named line in IX.11.
  - **Recommended:** (a). Perry's book is the church's own history, so it is own voice, not the independent study the rule needs. (c) would assume a Pentecostal identity the sources seen do not establish.

##### 6. SUP-6 — The Arab evangelical churches (Egypt's Synod of the Nile, the National Evangelical Synod of Syria and Lebanon, the Arab Baptists) and the Middle East Council of Churches

- **Kind:** church family (Arab Presbyterian, Baptist and Anglican churches), with one regional ecumenical council
- **Dates:** 1920-c. 2010. 1920: the evangelical churches of Syria and Lebanon reorganized as one synod [S, church source]. Inside the window: the Near East School of Theology, Beirut, 1932 [S]; the national synod takes charge of the mission's work, 1956 [S]; Egypt's synod autonomous 1957 and independent 1958 (PC(USA) source, V-s; a 1957 Christianity Today report of the vote, V-s); the Middle East Council of Churches' inaugural assembly, Nicosia, 28-30 May 1974 (MECC page, V); the Catholic family joins at the fifth assembly, January 1990 (V-s).
- **Region / countries:** Middle East; Africa (Egypt, as IX.10 is tagged) — Egypt, Lebanon, Syria, Jordan
- **Lane:** 6 Protestant & Evangelical
- **Section A:** Not yet assessable. Per body: the synods hold the Westminster standards [S]; no dated Arabic statement was read. The MECC is a council, not a confession.
- **B1:** Moderate. Heather J. Sharkey, American Evangelicals in Egypt (Princeton, 2008; V), on the mission and its Egyptian church to 1967, using Arabic sources. Habib Badr, ed., Christianity: A History in the Middle East (Beirut: MECC, 2005; English of المسيحية عبر تاريخها في المشرق; V-s), regional scholarship by 41 authors, 29 from the region. George Sabra, Truth and Service (2009), a NEST history [S]. Arabic church histories: wanted.
- **B2:** Good: Arabic hymnody and preaching, schools and hospitals (Assiut, Beirut), the Coptic Evangelical Organization for Social Services [S], minority life beside larger Orthodox and Catholic churches, the Lebanese civil war, ecumenical work with Orthodox churches.
- **B3:** Mid-high. The roster has Arab Christians in era 10 only through E5-E7 candidates (Orthodox, Maronite, Palestinian) and IX.10 (Coptic). No row carries Arab Protestants; IX.46 holds Geneva, not the MECC.
- **B4:** Arab and diaspora Protestants, historians of mission, ecumenists, readers asking what Protestant Christianity looks like in Arabic.
- **B5:** Small to mid; tiebreak only.
- **Tier:** Tier 3 now; Tier 2 with an Arabic own text and a Levant study
- **Evidence:**
  - Heather J. Sharkey, American Evangelicals in Egypt: Missionary Encounters in an Age of Empire (Princeton: Princeton University Press, 2008), xvi + 318 pp. — *scholarly study* — **V** — <https://cambridge.org/core/journals/church-history/article/american-evangelicals-in-egypt-missionary-encounters-in-an-age-of-empire-by-heather-sharkey-princeton-nj-princeton-university-press-2008-xvi-318-pp-4500-cloth/4A03D7F728265C4AD213E317A6BF9CBE> — Review by Akram F. Khater, Church History 80 (2011): 956, opened.
  - Habib Badr (chief ed.), Christianity: A History in the Middle East (Beirut: MECC Studies and Research Program, 2005); Arabic original المسيحية عبر تاريخها في المشرق, al-Masīḥiyya ʿabra tārīkhihā fī al-Mashriq ('Christianity through its history in the East') — *regional scholarship (Arabic original)* — **V-s** — <https://scholarhub.balamand.edu.lb/handle/uob/249> — Catalog and review seen in search results; the catalog page tried returned 404. Arabic edition date not confirmed.
  - Middle East Council of Churches, 'The Birth of the Middle East Council of Churches' (mecc.org, 13 August 2020): inaugural assembly, Nicosia, 28-30 May 1974 — *own text (later account of a dated founding)* — **V** — <https://www.mecc.org/mecc/2020/8/13/the-middle-east-council-of-churches-inaugural-assembly> — Page opened; names the evangelical members of the planning committee (Hovhannes Aharonian, Ibrahim Dagher, Fayez Fares).
  - Synod of the Nile autonomy 1957, independence 1958 — *dated events* — **V-s** — <https://www.presbyterianmission.org/?p=2700> — Church partner page and a 1957 Christianity Today report in search results.
  - Arabic-language histories of the Evangelical Church of Egypt and of the Syria-Lebanon synod; a dated Arabic statement of faith — *own-language sources* — **wanted** — An Arabic search returned only 2026 news of the synod's elections.
- **Living today:** True; newest dated evidence: 2026 (V-s); Al-Dostor and Akhbar el-Yom, 2026: the Synod of the Nile's 134th session elections (journalism, V-s); MECC's own pages, 2020-2025. Grade: CR.
- **Overlap:** IX.10 (Coptic revival; Egypt). E5 (Antiochian Orthodox; MECC pages are its sources). E7 (Palestinian Christians, incl. Anglican and Lutheran). IX.46 (WCC). The Armenian Evangelical churches of the Near East (union of 1924 [S]) were not researched and sit at the edge of IX.27.
- **Recommendation:** hold for sources (one Arabic own text and a Levant study); meanwhile the MECC as a named line in IX.46. (class: hold)

- **Kind:** four named lines, one per receiver row
- **Dates:** 1911-c. 2010 (various). Aymara Adventists: Ferdinand Stahl at Platería, Puno, 1911, joining Manuel Camacho's school at Utawilaya (Adventist and Peruvian journal accounts; V-s). Proaño bishop of Riobamba 1954-1985 (UASB thesis, V-s); the Escuelas Radiofónicas Populares del Ecuador, 1962 (V-s; some sources 1960). FEINE founded 1980 (V-s). The SIL in Peru from 1946 and Ecuador [S].

David Stoll, Fishers of Men or Founders of Empire? (Zed, 1982/83; V-s). Charles Teel on the Stahls (Spectrum 1987; Adventist Heritage 1988; V-s). No row-level study of Andean Christianity as one subject.
- **B2:** Rich but scattered.
- **B3:** Each line fills a real gap in its receiver.
- **B4:** Indigenous and Latin American Christians, historians of mission.
- **B5:** n/a (lines).

Year not confirmed.

Proaño's own writings (for example Creo en el hombre y en la comunidad, 1977 [S]) are wanted.
